# Sūrah al-Māʾidah (Chapter 5) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah of the Table**

Al-Māʾidah, "the Table," is the fifth chapter of the Quran, comprising 112 verses, and it takes its name from the table spread with food that the disciples of Jesus ask to be sent down from Heaven near the end of the sūrah. It stands as the longest of the legislative sūrahs in the Book, a sūrah whose opening addresses the believers directly with the command to honour their obligations, and whose course runs from that command through the law of the food and the covenant of the people, into the long account of the prophets, and out into the final account of the messengers before God. The sūrah is counted among the Medinan revelations, and most of its verses carry the weight of the community: the law of the sacrifice, the law of the bequest, the law of the witness, the law of the oath, the law of the fast, and the law of the pilgrimage are all kept within it, and the sūrah is the place in the Book where the details of the community's life are given in the most complete form.

**The Shape of the Sūrah**

The opening verses (1–12) state the case of the covenant: the command to honour the obligations, the lawfulness of the good food, the prohibition of what is not pure, and the account of the covenant of the prophets and the covenant of the nation, and the closing of the sūrah of the community in the state of the completion of the religion. The sūrah then turns to the narrative of the past (13–48): the children of Israel, the breach of the covenant, the calf, the sabbath, and the long account of Moses and his people, which is kept in the sūrah in the form of the warning to the community that has received the Book. The middle of the sūrah (49–85) keeps the law of the community in the state of the details: the oath of the kinship, the law of the food, the law of the fast and the pilgrimage, the oaths and the atonement of the oath, and the questions of the inquirers. The last part of the sūrah (86–120) returns to the account of the prophets and the messengers: the questioning of God to the messengers on the day of the judgment, the address of God to Jesus, the account of the table, the answer of Jesus to the question of the divinity, the intercession of Jesus for his people, and the final statement of the reward of the truthful and the sovereignty of God over the heavens and the earth.

**The Method of the Sūrah**

What distinguishes al-Māʾidah from its neighbours is the combination of the law and the narrative in the single account. The sūrah does not keep the law in the state of the list, and it does not keep the narrative in the state of the story; it keeps the law in the state of the reminder of the covenant, and it keeps the narrative in the state of the warning to the community that has received the Book. The opening command to honour the obligations is the form that the sūrah does in the state of the calling of the community to the keeping of the covenant, and the closing statement of the sovereignty of God is the form that the sūrah does in the state of the putting of the community in the hand of God, and between the two, the sūrah does in the form of the giving of the law and the telling of the story in the single account of the community and its God.


## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:1
> O believers! Honour your obligations. All grazing livestock has been made lawful to you—except what is hereby announced to you and hunting while on pilgrimage. Indeed, Allah commands what He wills.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command Before the Law**

The sūrah opens not with a story, a warning, or a statute, but with an obligation: fulfil your obligations. The word ʿuqūd is a plural — pacts, covenants, pledges — and most commentators read the command as the religious commitments the believers have made with God, in the same register as “Fulfil your covenant and I will fulfil Mine” (2:40) and “Honour Allah’s covenant when you make a pledge” (16:91). A minority read it more broadly, as an obligation that also binds the People of the Book, and the evidence they cite is a letter. The early ḥadīth scholar al-Zuhrī (d. 124/741) is reported to have seen a letter the Prophet sent to the Christians of Najrān, and the letter opened with these four verses, which is to say the text functioned, in the earliest period at least, as an address extending beyond the Muslim community. The structure is deliberate either way. The community keeps its promises first, and the law that will be enumerated in what follows is the substance of the promise. A list of foods standing alone would be a manual; a list that follows a command to keep one’s covenants is a document of the covenant itself.

**The Provision of the Steppe**

The second sentence moves from the abstract to the pasture, and grazing livestock is declared lawful to the believers. The term bahīmat al-anʿām names the animals that carry life across the Arabian landscape — camels, cattle, sheep — and, in the reports recorded by al-Ṭabarī, it extends to the young of the animals, and even to the unborn when the mother is slaughtered. The early exegetes argued about one category: the wild grazing animals, the deer among them. One side held that the term includes them, because it does not specify domestication. The other side placed game under a different law, the law of the hunt, which carries its own conditions and its own exceptions. The argument is not pedantic, because the two laws differ in kind. The pasture gives the animal; the hunt takes it. The verse itself hints at the distinction by naming hunting in its final clause, and the default position of the whole sentence is generosity. The ordinary food of the land is not rationed. It is given, and the giving is the first act of the law.

**The Exceptions Named in Advance**

“Except what is hereby announced to you” (5:1) is a forward pointer, and the announcement arrives in verse 3, where the forbidden are detailed: carrion, flowing blood, swine, what is slaughtered in the name of another, what dies by strangling, beating, a fall, or goring, and what the predator has torn. The parallel in the sūrah of al-Ḥajj carries the same structure, with the cattle made lawful “except what has ˹already˺ been recited to you,” immediately after the command that “whoever honours the rituals of Allah, it is best for them in the sight of their Lord” (22:30). One detail the commentators noticed is the placement of swine: it stands in the exception list of verse 3 and not here, because swine is not a grazing animal in the technical sense of bahīmat al-anʿām. The two verses together form a complete map of the food law. The general rule comes first, then the exceptions, each named with its own wording, and nothing in the law is left to be guessed. The naming is the announcement the first verse promises, and the promise is kept two verses later, in full.

**The Pilgrim Must Not Hunt**

The final restriction concerns the body rather than the plate. A person in the state of pilgrimage, iḥrām, is forbidden to hunt or to kill game. Iḥrām consecrates the body for worship: the pilgrim wears the unsewn garments, refrains from cutting hair or nails, and, by the same logic, refrains from taking the life of game within the sanctuary. The sūrah was revealed in the context of the Prophet’s Farewell Pilgrimage (10 AH / 632 CE), and this restriction is one of the standing rules the pilgrimage carries with it. Sea creatures remain outside the prohibition: “It is lawful for you to hunt and eat seafood” (5:96), and the ban on hunting on land while in iḥrām does not reach the water. The rule draws a line between two relations to the animal world. The hunter takes for himself; the pilgrim stands inside a boundary of consecration, and the suspension of the hunt is the visible sign that the boundary is real. The game the pilgrim might have killed stays alive, and the preserved life of the creature becomes part of the worship rather than its opposite.

**A Law That Is a Decree**

The closing clause, “Indeed, Allah commands what He wills” (5:1), adds no new rule. It restates the source of every rule in the verse. The permission to eat the grazing livestock, the announcement of the exceptions, the suspension of the hunt: none of these is a concession negotiated with the community, nor a compromise with custom. Each is a decree that carries the authority of the one who made the land and the animals in it. The Book pairs the enumeration of the lawful with a reminder that the enumeration is an act of will, and the pairing recurs across the dietary laws. The reader is meant to receive the food law not as a menu but as a gift from a sovereign, and a gift received as a gift is kept. The first verse of the sūrah is a complete argument in miniature: the covenant is the form, the pasture is the content, the hunting ban is the boundary, and the sovereignty of the decree is the ground on which the whole construction stands.

**Why the Order Is the Argument**

The arrangement of the verse carries its own meaning. The sūrah that opens with a list of foods would be a manual; the sūrah that opens with an obligation and only then gives the list is a covenant document. The obligations are fulfilled by keeping what follows, and what follows is the substance of the covenant at the material level: what may be eaten, what may not, when the hunt is suspended, and who stands behind the permission. The later verses of the sūrah will move from the plate to the court, to the synagogue, to the church, to the battlefield, but they remain inside the frame the first verse sets. The binding and the giving hold the whole sūrah together, and every later verse in it stands, one way or another, on that first command to keep the promises already made.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:2
> O believers! Do not violate Allah’s rituals ˹of pilgrimage˺, the sacred months, the sacrificial animals, the ˹offerings decorated with˺ garlands, nor those ˹pilgrims˺ on their way to the Sacred House seeking their Lord’s bounty and pleasure. When pilgrimage has ended, you are allowed to hunt. Do not let the hatred of a people who once barred you from the Sacred Mosque provoke you to transgress. Cooperate with one another in goodness and righteousness, and do not cooperate in sin and transgression. And be mindful of Allah. Surely Allah is severe in punishment.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Proper Name for the Sacred**

The verse opens with a term the Qur’an uses with rare precision: the rituals of Allah (shaʿāʾir). The word appears in the Qur’an only in connection with the Makkah pilgrimage. Ṣafā and Marwah are “among the symbols of Allah” (2:158); “whoever honours the symbols of Allah, it is certainly out of the piety of the heart” (22:32); and the sacrificial camels are “among the symbols of Allah” (22:36). Here the term is deployed as a list: the rituals, the months, the animals, the garlands, the travellers on the road to the House. Each item is a boundary of the sacred, and the command is not to violate any of them. The exegetes understood the prohibition in three layers: to recognise the rituals as sacred, to observe everything the pilgrim’s state forbids, and to perform every element of the rite. Some extended the term further, reading it as a general command to honour all divine commands and prohibitions, but the Qur’an itself confines the word to the pilgrimage, and the list here is a list of pilgrimage things.

**Four Months Frozen Against War**

The singular “the sacred month” refers, on the reading of most commentators, to all four months in which the pre-Islamic Arabs suspended warfare: Rajab, Dhu’l-Qa’dah, Dhu’l-Hijjah, and Muharram. The Book confirms the number elsewhere: “of which four are sacred” (9:36). A smaller question, which single month the singular designates, produced three answers: Dhu’l-Hijjah, the month of the great pilgrimage; Rajab, the month most associated with the old truces; or Dhu’l-Qa’dah. The deeper legal question is whether the sanctity survives the command to fight the polytheists wherever found (9:5). Some exegetes answered that the fighting ruling abrogates the truce, at least in relation to the idolaters. But this verse, its parallel in verse 97, and 9:36 all belong to the late period of the revelation, and the Prophet upheld the months in his Farewell address, so the weight of the evidence points to a sanctity that was preserved. The months remain sacred; the permission to fight within them is the exception that confirms the rule.

**The Stolen Camels and the Garlands**

The report the commentators attach to the verse is a small lesson in the separation of crimes and rituals. A man from Yamāmah had come to Madinah, feigned interest in Islam, and left without any commitment, leading away camels that belonged to the Muslims. After the conquest of Makkah, in the brief period before the polytheists were barred from the pilgrimage, the same man was seen approaching the Sacred House, driving the stolen camels, garlands around their necks marking them as offerings for sacrifice. The Muslims debated whether to stop him. The answer was that they must not interfere: the garlands made the animals offerings, and an offering on its way to the House was inviolable, whatever its owner had done. The ruling does not condone the theft; it draws a line between the criminal and the ritual object. The sacred thing keeps its protection even when the one who handles it has no right to it, and the law guards the symbol while the crime is dealt with on its own terms.

**The Pilgrim’s Right of Passage**

Those who are on their way to the Sacred House are given a right of passage, and the verse describes them as *seeking their Lord’s bounty and pleasure*. The two nouns matter. The pilgrim may seek God’s pleasure in worship and, in the same journey, the worldly gain of the trade conducted in the sacred season, because the four months included the trading fairs and the pilgrimage was always also an economy. The Qur’an does not purify the motive by refusing the second noun. It names both, and the law treats the journey as a single protected act. The protection is personal: a pilgrim in transit is not to be obstructed, robbed, or turned back, whatever the politics of the last year have made of his people. A man may arrive at the House with a past that the community still resents, and the verse insists that the road to the House is not the place to settle that resentment.

**Old Hatred, New Neighbours**

The people “who once barred you from the Sacred Mosque” (5:2) are the Meccans of the ʿumrah turned back at al-Ḥudaybiyyah in 6 AH / 628, and the same community that had driven the Muslims from their homes. Between that humiliation and this verse lay the conquest, and the sworn enemies had become the newest members of the community in a matter of days. The warning is aimed at the moment of the power change: the temptation to settle old scores is greatest exactly when the old enemy has become a neighbour and a fellow pilgrim. The Prophet’s own conduct in the conquest, a general amnesty with no blood taken for what had been done, is the practice of this verse, and the verse gives that practice its name. The hatred of a people is not a ground for transgression, and a community that punishes through its new neighbours is a community that has not yet digested the peace.

**Cooperation as a Standing Command**

The verse ends by leaving the sanctuary and issuing a rule for the whole community: “Cooperate with one another in goodness and righteousness, and do not cooperate in sin and transgression” (5:2). It is one of the very few verses that make cooperation itself a duty, and its placement here is significant. The law of the pilgrimage requires many people to act in concert — the caravans, the sacrifices, the fairs — and the verse generalises the requirement beyond the ritual. The negative half is as binding as the positive: to assist in a crime is a crime of the same kind, and the law does not let the helper hide behind the word *assisting*. The closing warning, “Surely Allah is severe in punishment” (5:2), lands on the whole list — the violated rituals, the broken months, the obstructed pilgrim, the hatred acted out, the cooperation in sin — and it is the only place in the verse where severity appears. The severity guards the borders, while the worship itself remains what it has always been: a gift.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:3
> Forbidden to you are carrion, blood, and swine; what is slaughtered in the name of any other than Allah; what is killed by strangling, beating, a fall, or by being gored to death; what is partly eaten by a predator unless you slaughter it; and what is sacrificed on altars. You are also forbidden to draw lots for decisions. This is all evil. Today the disbelievers have given up all hope of ˹undermining˺ your faith. So do not fear them; fear Me! Today I have perfected your faith for you, completed My favour upon you, and chosen Islam as your way. But whoever is compelled by extreme hunger— not intending to sin—then surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Most Detailed List of Forbidden Food**

The Qur’an contains several dietary statutes — 2:173, 6:145, 16:115 — and this is the most detailed of them. The earlier lists name the categories: carrion, blood, swine, what is slaughtered in the name of other than God. This list keeps those four and then adds the modes of death that disqualify an animal: strangling, beating, a fall from a height, being gored, and being torn by a predator. It is also, by the account of most commentators, the last of the food statutes to be revealed, which is to say the law arrived in its final form at the very end of the revelation. The effect of the detail is certainty. A Muslim at the close of the Prophet’s life is left with no case he can plausibly claim not to have understood. Every way the animal can die and become unclean is named, and the naming is the announcement the first verse of the sūrah promised.

**Why the Manner of Death Matters**

The law treats the death of the animal as the thing that decides its lawfulness, and the exegetes attached a reason to each item. Strangling was known in Arabia both as a deliberate practice of some idolaters and as an accidental death, and the law forbids it in both forms. Beating to death was a method of killing that pre-Islamic groups used to preserve the flesh, and a ḥadīth extends the principle: an animal struck dead by a blunt arrow without being pierced counts as beaten to death and may not be eaten. A fall from a height and a goring death stand as they are, whether accidental or caused. The animal torn by a predator is forbidden only up to a point: the exception *that which you may purify* (dhakkaytum) covers the beast a predator has wounded but that a person reaches in time to slaughter properly before it dies. The verb comes from a root the grammarians connect with fragrance and purity, and the connection is not ornamental: the meat of a properly slaughtered animal, its blood drained, is the meat that keeps. The exception does not reach carrion or swine, which are forbidden absolutely.

**Altars, Arrows, and the Divining of Fate**

The last two items in the list belong to the cult rather than to the kitchen. What is sacrificed on the altars (ʿalā nuṣub) refers to the stone shrines of pre-Islamic Arabia, at which the polytheists slaughtered and dedicated their animals; nuṣub can also be read as idols, and the prohibition covers the dedication in either form. The drawing of lots for decisions refers to the azlām, the divining arrows the polytheists used to settle questions of law and custom — to ask the arrows whether a marriage was permitted, a journey wise, a sale valid. The verse names the practice as evil, and verse 90 of this same sūrah will forbid the arrows again, alongside intoxicants and gambling. The point of the two items together is that the community’s decisions are to be made by revelation and by reason, not by the fall of a stick. A religion that outsources its verdicts to chance has surrendered the very faculty for which the law is meant to be a discipline.

**The Day the Despair Began**

Most commentators date the verse to the Day of ʿArafah, on a Friday, during the Prophet’s Farewell Sermon, and for that reason they count it among the last verses received. The others in that small group are 2:281, 4:176, 9:128–129, and the sūrah of an-Nasr (110); a minority placed the verse at the conquest of Makkah. A later, sectarian layer sits on the dating. In Twelver Shiʿi tradition the verse is sometimes placed at Ghadīr Khumm, where the Prophet is reported to have said of ʿAlī, “For whomever I am his master, ʿAlī is his master,” and the perfection of the religion is then read as the establishment of the imāmah. The Sunni exegetes record the event but reject the reading of the verse. What all the accounts agree on is that the despair of the disbelievers is real and dated: on that day the possibility that the Muslims would be turned from their faith closed, and the verse announces the closing as a fact of history rather than as a prediction.

**Three Verbs for a Completed Religion**

The declaration *I have perfected your faith for you, completed My favour upon you, and chosen Islam as your way* carries three verbs, and the exegetes divided over their object. One reading ties the perfection to the pilgrimage itself: the Farewell Hajj gave the community its final form of the last ritual pillar, and after that day no new rite was laid upon them. Another ties it to the revelation: the Book was complete, and the community had received everything it would receive. Al-Bayḍāwī offers a third position, that the perfection is the victory of Islam over the idolatry then expelled from the sanctuary, a victory that makes the completion of the blessing plausible. And Ṭabarī records a report that the Islam named here is not the verbal profession but the full submission of the heart to divine unity, a level above the declaration of the tongue. The verbs differ in their object but agree in their direction: the religion of the community is finished as a construction, and standing as a gift.

**Hunger, Compulsion, and the Limits of the Law**

The verse ends by returning to the plate. One who is compelled by extreme hunger, without intending to sin, may eat of the forbidden, and God is *All-Forgiving, Most Merciful*. The condition is stated twice, and both parts matter: the compulsion is hunger, not mere appetite, and the intention is not to sin — the person eats to live, not to transgress. The parallel in 2:173 gives the same exception, and the general law of necessity covers the rest. The placement of the clause at the end of the verse is a statement about the character of the law. The prohibition is absolute, but the person inside the prohibition is not. A law with no door for the starving would be a law that eats its own subjects, and this verse refuses to be that. The mercy is not an addendum to the statute; it is built into the statute, and it is what makes the statute a law rather than a trap.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:4
> They ask you, ˹O Prophet,˺ what is permissible for them ˹to eat˺. Say, “What is good and lawful. Also what is caught by your hunting animals and birds of prey which you have trained as instructed by Allah. So eat what they catch for you, but mention the Name of Allah over it ˹first˺.” And be mindful of Allah. Surely Allah is swift in reckoning.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question That Shapes the Answer**

The verse opens in the middle of a conversation: “They ask you, O Prophet, what is permissible for them to eat” (5:4). The questioners are not named, and the exegetes leave them unnamed, because the structure of the answer does not depend on their identity. The question is about food, and the answer it receives is the widest statement the Book makes about the diet of the community: “What is good and lawful.” A community that has just received the detailed list of the forbidden in the previous verse is now given the default rule. The good things are lawful, full stop, and the statute of verse 3 stands as the named exception to that default. The question is real in the sense that the early Muslims did ask what they could eat under the new law, and the answer reorients the whole dietary system from restriction to permission. The law is not a wall with a few doors; it is an open field with a few marked boundaries.

**Lawfulness as the Default, Restriction as the Exception**

The word for the good things (ṭayyibāt) runs through the Qur’an as a positive category of divine provision. “So eat from the good, lawful things which Allah has provided for you” (16:114) is the standing invitation, and the Book turns the same category against those who do the opposite. “Ask, O Prophet, ‘Who has forbidden the adornments and lawful provisions Allah has brought forth for His servants?’” (7:32), and “Do not falsely declare with your tongues, ‘This is lawful, and that is unlawful,’ fabricating lies against Allah” (16:116). The Qur’an criticises both those who eat what is forbidden (5:42, 62–63) and those who declare forbidden what God made lawful (5:87, 7:32, 10:59, 16:116). The Israelite background sharpens the point: “All food was lawful for the children of Israel, except what Israel made unlawful for himself before the Torah was revealed” (3:93), and Jesus is said to have come “to confirm the Torah revealed before me and legalize some of what had been forbidden to you” (3:50). The present verse stands at the end of that movement: the good things are lawful, and the ones who close them off are the ones who need to be told why.

**The Trained Beast and the Untrained Beast**

The second part of the answer concerns the hunt. A hunting animal — the dog, the falcon, the trained bird — is a partner in the acquisition of food, but only on a condition, and the condition is training. The teaching has two parts, and both come out of the verse: the animal obeys the call of its master, and it catches the prey without eating it. The training is described as *as instructed by Allah*, which places the human teacher inside the same order as the animal. The skill is given, and the one who has received it passes it on. The Name of God is pronounced before the animal is released, or over the prey if it is still alive when it is reached. The untrained beast, the wild beast, the animal that eats its catch, stands outside the permission, and the reasons for the exclusion are drawn out in what follows. The line between the trained and the untrained is the line between an instrument of the law and a force of nature.

**When the Prey Is Disqualified**

The exegetes drew the line between the lawful catch and the unlawful one with a precision that does not depend on the quality of the meat. If the prey is taken by a wild animal or by an animal of unknown training, it is not to be eaten, because the law cannot verify the training and will not presume it. If the hunting animal has eaten part of the prey, the catch is disqualified — not as a matter of impurity, but as a matter of evidence. The animal that eats its catch has not been taught the condition the verse requires, or it caught the prey for itself rather than for its master, and either fact puts the meat outside the permission. The one escape is the exception established in verse 3: if the hunter reaches the prey before it dies and slaughters it properly, the Name is mentioned over it, and the meat is lawful. The law is not a wall; it is a boundary with a door, and the door is the proper slaughter that verse 3 already provided.

**The Name Over the Catch**

The closing instruction — mention the Name of Allah over it — places the act of naming at the moment the prey becomes food. In the law of slaughter, the Name is said over the animal at the killing; in the law of the hunt, it is said at the release or at the catch. The effect is the same. The meat enters the community through an act of consecration, and the absence of the act leaves it outside. The connection to 6:121 is direct: “Do not eat of what is not slaughtered in Allah’s Name.” The hunted animal is not less an animal than the slaughtered one, and the law does not let it pass into the pot without the same word of invocation. The hunter stands in the same relation to the creature as the one who slaughters: he takes a life that is not his to take, and the Name over the catch is the acknowledgment of that fact. The kill becomes a meal only when it is named, and the naming is what turns a piece of flesh into a provision.

**Swift in Reckoning**

The verse ends with a warning that matches the permission it contains: “Surely Allah is swift in reckoning” (5:4). The balance of the verse is the balance of the whole dietary law — a wide permission, a named exception, a single condition, and a closing reminder that the permission is a trust. To eat the good things is the norm; to cross into the forbidden is a transgression that will be accounted for, and the reckoning is described as swift, which is to say it is not postponed. The verse gives the community its food, and it gives the community its accountability, in the same breath. A law that handed out provisions without keeping accounts would be a law for the unaccountable, and this verse refuses to be one.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:5
> Today all good, pure foods have been made lawful for you. Similarly, the food of the People of the Book is permissible for you and yours is permissible for them. And ˹permissible for you in marriage˺ are chaste believing women as well as chaste women of those given the Scripture before you—as long as you pay them their dowries in wedlock, neither fornicating nor taking them as mistresses. And whoever rejects the faith, all their good deeds will be void ˹in this life˺ and in the Hereafter they will be among the losers.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Second “Today” for the Community**

The verse opens with the same word that carried the declaration of perfection in verse 3: “Today all good, pure foods have been made lawful for you” (5:5). The repetition is not a slip of style; it is the sūrah’s way of marking a single day as a turning point. The triumphal tone of the previous verse carries over. The community that has just been told its religion is perfected is now told its table is open, and the lawfulness of the good things is placed inside the event of the community’s completion. Some exegetes took *today* in the wider sense — not the day of the revelation but the era of Islam, the period in which the law stands. Either way the function is the same. The food law is one of the marks of the completed community, and the verse makes the table a sign of the same event that made the religion whole.

**The Table Shared with the People of the Book**

The second sentence opens a door the previous verse had left closed: “the food of the People of the Book is permissible for you and yours is permissible for them” (5:5). The majority reading concerns the meat slaughtered by Jews and Christians, whose slaughter is governed by a revelation the Qur’an recognises. An early Shiʿi commentary took a narrower view, limiting the license to foods that required no slaughter at all — vegetables, grains, fruits. The majority reports run in the other direction, and they are broad: some held that even food presented as an offering in a church, or slaughtered in the name of the Messiah, is lawful to the Muslim. Against that breadth, two Companions are reported to have drawn a personal line. ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib and ʿĀʾishah held that if a Muslim hears the name of other than God invoked at the slaughter, he should not eat the meat, and their caution rests on the command “Do not eat of what is not slaughtered in Allah’s Name” (6:121). The license is real and wide, and the line it does not cross is the invocation of another name over the animal.

**The Door of Marriage and Its Locks**

The third sentence extends the license to the household: “chaste believing women as well as chaste women of those given the Scripture before you” (5:5). The Arabic muḥṣanāt can mean chaste women or free women, and the exegetes divided over which sense the verse intends. On the chaste reading, the word describes the woman’s present status, not her past: a woman who had acted immorally and repented regained the status and was eligible. ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb is reported to have warned that a woman’s past, once repented, was not to be raised against her in a marriage proposal. On the free reading, the permission covers only free women of the People of the Book, since the explicit permission to marry believing slave women comes from other verses (2:221, 4:25). Either way, the locks are stated in the verse itself. The dowry is to be paid *in wedlock*, and the relationship is not to be *neither fornicating nor taking them as mistresses*. The marriage is a marriage, with its full legal form, and nothing less.

**How Far the License Reaches**

A question the early commentators took seriously was the scope of the two communities named. The majority reading is that the license covers all Jews and Christians, wherever they live and however imperfectly they observe their own law, not only the upright among them. The Zoroastrians complicate the edge. They held protected status under Islamic rule, but most jurists did not count them among the People of the Book, and the license to eat their food and marry their women is not given to them by this verse. Some later scholars stretched the term to include them; the majority did not. Within the Shiʿi tradition, a caution developed that discouraged the marriage even where the license allowed it, except in the absence of Muslim partners — a position that did not cancel the verse but qualified its practice. The license is a door the law opens, and the community’s own wisdom may choose when to walk through it.

**What the License Presumes**

The two licenses — food and marriage — presume a society in which the Abrahamic communities live together and exchange goods and spouses. The Qur’an forbids the Muslim to marry the polytheist: “Do not marry polytheistic women until they believe” (2:221), and 60:10 commands that one “do not hold on to marriage with polytheistic women.” The contrast is the point. The People of the Book stand in a different relation to the Muslim community than the idolaters do, and the verse gives that relation its social form. To eat at a Jew’s table, to marry a Christian woman, is to recognise a shared lineage of revelation. The license is not a neutral permission; it is an act of recognition, and the recognition runs in both directions, because the verse states that the food of the Muslims is permissible to them as well. The table is shared, and the sharing is the sign of the shared covenant.

**Deeds That Are Vain**

The verse ends by leaving the social world and stating the theological rule: “whoever rejects the faith, all their good deeds will be void and in the Hereafter they will be among the losers.” The statement is one of the Book’s standing formulas: without belief, the good deed has no reward in the next life. The parallels are numerous — 2:217, 6:88, 39:65 — and they state the principle in the same order. Its placement here, at the end of a verse of licenses, is a reminder that the table and the household are blessings for the believing community, and that the one who rejects the faith stands outside them, however good his deeds may appear to the eye. The loss is not in this life; it is in the Hereafter, and it is final. The verse opens the community’s doors and then closes the door of disbelief, and the two movements belong together.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:6
> O believers! When you rise up for prayer, wash your faces and your hands up to the elbows, wipe your heads, and wash your feet to the ankles. And if you are in a state of ˹full˺ impurity, then take a full bath. But if you are ill, on a journey, or have relieved yourselves, or have been intimate with your wives and cannot find water, then purify yourselves with clean earth by wiping your faces and hands. It is not Allah’s Will to burden you, but to purify you and complete His favour upon you, so perhaps you will be grateful.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Body in the Order of the Verse**

The verse lays out the ablution (wuḍūʾ) in the order the body is to be washed: the face, the hands to the elbows, the head wiped, and the feet to the ankles. The one point on which the early commentators divided is the feet. The phrase *your feet to the ankles* can be read as the object of the nearer verb — wipe — in which case the feet are wiped and not washed. It can be read as the object of the more distant verb — wash — in which case the wiping of the head is a self-contained act and the feet are washed. Ibn ʿAbbās and a number of the Companions preferred the wiping; the majority of the later schools preferred the washing, on the ground of the predominant practice of the Prophet. The report from Anas ibn Mālik tries to hold both together: on the plain sense the verse requires the wiping, while the practice of the Prophet is the washing. The two readings are sometimes reconciled by saying that the verse commands the wiping in form and the washing in substance, and the dispute is one of the small, durable disagreements the law carries without resolving it.

**A New Ablution for Every Prayer?**

The opening words — *when you rise up for prayer* — read, on their face, as a command to make a fresh ablution before every prayer, whatever the existing state of purity. The exegetes report that this was the ruling at the time of the revelation: a new wuḍū before each prayer, regardless of the state one was already in. The practice of the Prophet changed the application. After the conquest of Makkah, he is reported to have made one ablution before the midday prayer, and then prayed the prayers that followed without renewing it, and that single act was taken as the abrogation of the renewal. The ablution is now required only when the state of purity is broken — by sleep, by the relief of nature, by intercourse — and the renewal before each prayer remains a commendable act rather than an obligation. The law moved from the stricter reading of the verse to the gentler practice of the one to whom it was revealed, and the gentler reading is the one the community inherited.

**Three Purifications in a Single Verse**

The verse contains, in one compact passage, the three ways the Muslim purifies himself before prayer. The wuḍū is the partial washing for the minor impurity; the ghusl is the full bath for the major impurity, the state of menstruation, of intercourse, of seminal discharge; and the tayammum is the purification with clean earth for the one who cannot use water. The conditions for the tayammum are stated with a precision the law elsewhere does not always show: illness, a journey, the relief of nature, intimacy with the wife, and the absence of water. The same list appears in the parallel verse 4:43, which gives the same four triggers in the context of the prohibition on approaching prayer while intoxicated or in major impurity. The two verses together are the complete law of ritual purity: the washing when the water is there, the earth when it is not, and the bath for the states that the washing does not reach. One verse carries the whole system, and the system is built around the availability of water.

**The Schools’ Disputes**

The verse was the text on which the legal schools built their differing rulings on the ablution. The Mālikīs, Shāfiʿīs, Ḥanbalīs, and Jaʿfarīs all require a statement of intention (niyyah) before the washing begins, and all require that the washings be carried out in one continuous act. The Shāfiʿīs, Ḥanbalīs, and Jaʿfarīs add that each part be washed in the order the verse lays it down, and the Mālikīs add that the parts are to be rubbed rather than merely covered with water. A minority report, that the Prophet wiped over his shoes during the ablution, gave rise to the ruling that the shoes may be wiped, a practice the Jaʿfarī school accepts as the rule and the Sunni schools allow in the cold months of winter. The disputes are small in their material and large in their principle: the verse gives the skeleton of the rite, and the schools have argued, carefully and for centuries, over the flesh.

**Purification as a Favour, Not a Burden**

The closing clause states the theology of the whole law: “It is not Allah’s Will to burden you, but to purify you and complete His favour upon you, so perhaps you will be grateful” (5:6). The ablution is a means of ritual purification, not of moral purification, but the prophetic tradition binds the two together. The Prophet is reported to have said of one who performs the ablution well that his sins wash away from him, from every part of the body, and Ṣaīḥ Muslim (223) carries the stronger form: “Cleanliness is half of faith.” The completion of the favour echoes the completion of the favour in verse 3, and the two verses frame the law of the community. The religion is perfected, the purification is a favour, the burden is denied, and the response the law asks for is gratitude. A rite that is described as a favour is a rite that is meant to be kept gladly, and the verse makes gladness the measure of the worship.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:7
> Remember Allah’s favour upon you and the covenant He made with you when you said, “We hear and obey.” And be mindful of Allah. Surely Allah knows best what is ˹hidden˺ in the heart.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Favour Tied to a Covenant**

The verse pairs two things the community is commanded to remember: the favour of God and the covenant He made with it. The pairing is not accidental; it belongs to a small group of verses in this opening block — verse 3, verse 6, this verse, and verse 11 — in which the favour of God is invoked toward the end of the mission, and the verses together form a collective statement of special divine grace. Al-Rāzī draws a distinction that keeps the reminder from becoming routine. To remember the favour is not a command issued because the community had forgotten; it is a call to keep the memory alive at all times, so that the community’s growing strength and ease do not make the believers forget the source of what they have been given. A community that forgets the favour begins to treat it as its own achievement, and the verse is a guard against that forgetting.

**The Pledges of Allegiance as One Covenant**

The covenant the community is asked to remember is, in the majority reading, the body of pledges of allegiance (bayʿah) the community made with the Prophet. The pledges run from the earliest ones, concluded with the future Emigrants and Helpers on the eve of the migration in 1 AH / 622, through the renewal of the pledge on the outskirts of Makkah at al-Ḥudaybiyyah in 6 AH / 628, in the middle of the tense treaty negotiations, down to the individual pledges made to the Prophet to obey his guidance. The legal point that makes all of them a covenant with God is stated elsewhere in the Book. Whoever obeys the Messenger has truly obeyed Allah (4:80), and the verse about the Ḥudaybiyyah pledge is explicit: “Surely those who pledge allegiance to you ˹O Prophet˺ are actually pledging allegiance to Allah. Allah’s Hand is over theirs” (48:10). The pledge to the Prophet is therefore not a political act with a political object. It is an act of obedience whose true addressee is God, and the covenant of this verse is the sum of those acts.

**The Minor Reading: A Covenant Before Time**

A minority tradition, associated mainly with Mujāhid, reads the covenant as the primordial one: the testimony in which God brought forth the descendants of Adam and asked, “Am I not your Lord?” to which the answer was, “Yes, You are! We testify” (7:172). On that reading the verse reaches back before history, to the covenant every human being made before birth. The objection that ended its currency is straightforward. The verse is addressed to the Muslim community specifically, not to the children of Adam generally, and the very next stretch of the sūrah opens with the covenant of the Children of Israel and their breaking of it. The structure of the sūrah points one way: this is the covenant of this community, and it is to be remembered in contrast with the covenant of the community that came before and failed. The primordial covenant remains in the Book as a deeper layer of the same idea, but this verse lives on the historical level.

**“We Hear and We Obey”**

The verse records the community’s answer in two words: we hear and obey. The order of the two verbs is the point. Hearing comes first, because the command is received before it is carried out, and the answer names both stages of obedience. The formula recurs in the Book as the standard form of the believer’s reply to God’s word, and its simplicity is its strength. A community that hears and obeys does not negotiate, does not append conditions, does not postpone. The verse places those two words at the moment of the covenant, which is to say it defines the covenant by the answer it received. What binds the community is not the content of the pledge alone but the form of the consent: full hearing, full obedience, without reservation. The two words are the seal of the pact.

**The Warning About the Breasts**

The closing warning returns to a theme the Qur’an states again and again: God knows what is in the breasts. The parallel verses are numerous — 3:119, 8:43, 29:10, 39:7, 64:4, 67:13 among them — and the warning here lands on a specific point. The covenant can be broken in two places, and both are covered. It can be broken outwardly, by the hand that reneges on the pledge, and it can be broken inwardly, by the heart that keeps the pledge in form while withdrawing the consent in substance. A pledge made with the tongue and broken in the breast is a pledge God sees, because the breast is the place the verse names. The warning is not a surveillance doctrine; it is the completion of the covenant. The community pledged with its hearing and its obedience, and the verse reminds it that the covenant extends to the place where the hearing and the obedience are either true or not.

**The Frame of the Opening Block**

The verse sits at the hinge of the sūrah’s opening section. Verses 1 through 11 address the Muslim community as a whole, and this is the verse that binds the section together by naming the two things the community has in common with God: the favour received and the covenant kept. After it, the sūrah turns to the community that came before — the Children of Israel and their covenant, and the covenant of the Christians — and the contrast that follows can be understood only because this verse has fixed the Muslim covenant in the reader’s mind. The opening block is a statement of the community’s standing, and the standing rests on the two words the verse preserves: we hear and obey.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:8
> O believers! Stand firm for Allah and bear true testimony. Do not let the hatred of a people lead you to injustice. Be just! That is closer to righteousness. And be mindful of Allah. Surely Allah is All-Aware of what you do.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Standing for God and Standing for Justice**

The command to stand firm for God is joined in the same breath to the bearing of true testimony, and the joining is the verse’s first argument. The parallel in 4:135 carries the same connection in the other order: “Stand firm for justice as witnesses for Allah even if it is against yourselves, your parents, or close relatives.” Read together, the two verses establish that standing for God and standing for justice are not two duties but one. To bear witness to God’s oneness is to bear witness to the standard by which all claims are measured, and to measure a claim — a testimony, a case, a debt — by that standard is an act of standing for God. The verse does not ask the believer to choose between the altar and the courtroom. The courtroom is where the altar’s claim is enforced, and the one who bears true testimony under pressure is the one whose witness to God is real.

**Hatred Is Not a Ground for Injustice**

The verse then names the specific corruption that the command must resist: the hatred of a people. The warning is not abstract, because the community it addresses had just lived through the hatred — the years of the Meccan bar from the Sacred Mosque, the battles, the conquest, and the sudden transformation of the sworn enemies into the newest neighbours. Verse 2 already warned that the hatred of a people was not to be allowed to lead into transgression, and this verse extends the same warning from the sanctuary into the whole domain of justice. To be just toward an enemy means, in the exegetes’ concrete list, not to kill their women and children, not to break the treaties made with them, not to take what the law does not give. The force of the command shows in its hardest case: if injustice toward those the believer is commanded to hate is forbidden, then injustice toward the believer’s own kin is forbidden all the more. The a fortiori argument is the verse’s second argument, and it closes the door on every version of tribal justice that protects the in-group and preys on the out-group.

**Justice as Nearer to Taqwā**

The verse makes a claim about geometry: justice is nearer to righteousness (taqwā). The language of nearness is the Qur’an’s way of describing the path, and it appears in the verse that follows the fall, where the believer who does good is told that the path to God is the one he walks. The claim here is that taqwā is not a destination reached by ritual alone. It is the quality of the person who stands in the right place when standing in the right place costs something. The believer who gives a true testimony against his own interest is, at that moment, closer to God than the believer whose prayer is unburdened by any such cost. Justice is nearer to taqwā because it is the place where taqwā is tested, and a virtue that has not been tested by an adversary is a virtue that has not yet been found.

**Witness as an Act of Worship**

The bearing of testimony is treated in the verse as a form of standing for God, which means the sūrah places the act of witnessing inside the circle of worship. The testimony is not a social service performed in addition to the ritual obligations; it is the ritual obligation performed in the social world. The believer who comes forward to give evidence, who tells the truth when the truth is inconvenient, is doing the same work the prayer does, in the court instead of in the mosque. This is why the command is addressed to the community in the plural, and why the warning follows in the same verse. The community is the body that bears the witness, and the witness is the body’s way of standing for God while the community is doing its ordinary business. The verse refuses the division that would let the believer worship God on the weekday and be unjust on the weekend.

**Allah Is All-Aware of What You Do**

The closing clause places the whole command under a single watch: Allah is All-Aware of what you do. The awareness covers the act and the motive together. The testimony given and the hatred that shaped it are both within the view, and the verse that preceded this one had already named the breast as the place God knows. Justice that is performed in the open and motivated by the hatred it pretends to set aside is seen as it is, not as it appears. The awareness is the condition that makes the command work. A command to be just that the believer could violate unseen would be a command for the brave; the verse adds the watcher, and the watcher makes the command a command for everyone, in every case, including the case where no other watcher is present.

**A Command for the Moment of Power**

The placement of the verse, immediately after the reminder of the covenant and the favour, gives it its historical force. The community has just passed from the weakest position it ever held to a position of power over the people it once hated, and the sūrah issues its sharpest command at exactly that moment. The temptation to be just is greatest when justice costs nothing, and the verse addresses the moment when justice first costs something real: the testimony that harms the in-group, the ruling that favours the former enemy, the restraint that forgives what the law would permit. The command to stand firm for God and bear true testimony is the sūrah’s answer to the moment of power, and the answer is that power is the test, not the reward.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:9
> Allah has promised those who believe and do good ˹His˺ forgiveness and a great reward.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Promise in Its Simplest Form**

The verse is one of the shortest in the sūrah, and its shortness is a feature. It contains three elements and nothing else: an address to the believers, a condition stated in a single clause, and a promise stated in a single clause. There is no story, no example, no elaboration. The promise is made the way a law is made — directly, in the voice of God, with the object of the promise named precisely. This is the form of the Book’s standing promises, the ones that carry the weight of the doctrine without the ornament of narrative, and the verse belongs to that class. Its economy is not a lack of content; it is the content. The community that has been told, in the opening block of the sūrah, to keep its covenant, to bear true testimony, and to let hatred lead it nowhere, is now told what those things are worth, and the answer is given without padding.

**Forgiveness Comes Before the Reward**

The promise names two things, and the order in which it names them is the verse’s quiet argument. Forgiveness comes first, and the great reward second. The order states a theology: the reward is not reached except through the forgiveness, and the forgiveness is what makes the reward possible. The one who believes and does good still carries the residue of the failures that belong to the condition of being a person, and the promise begins with the removal of that residue rather than with the addition of the prize. The Book states the same order elsewhere. “If you avoid the major sins forbidden to you, We will absolve you of your ˹lesser˺ misdeeds and admit you into a place of honour” (4:31), and “For Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful” closes the verse that turns evil deeds into good ones (25:70). The forgiveness is the precondition and the reward the consequence, and the verse keeps the two in the order in which they actually occur.

**The Condition in Two Words**

The condition of the promise is stated in the pair that runs through the entire Qur’an: those who believe and do good. The two words — belief and the good deeds — are never separated in the Book’s promises, and the separation is always a mistake. Belief without the deeds is a claim the deeds do not confirm, and the deeds without the belief are a work that has no one to receive it. The verse that closed the previous block of the sūrah stated the negative side of the same rule: whoever rejects the faith, all their good deeds will be void. Read against that, the condition of the promise is complete. It is not belief alone that is promised the reward, and it is not the deeds alone, but the two held together, in the order in which the verse states them. The pair is the standard formula, and the verse uses it without any addition, which is to say the formula is the whole condition.

**The Reward Left Undescribed**

The second noun of the promise — a great reward — is left deliberately indefinite. The sūrah elsewhere spells out the content of the reward in its full, described form, the Gardens under which rivers flow, and the community of the sūrah’s closing verses is promised exactly that. Here the reward is named only by its magnitude. The reason for the difference is the function of the verse. It is a promise made at the threshold of the warning, in the middle of the opening block, and its job is to state that the promise exists and is great, not to describe the place where it is kept. The description comes where the sūrah can carry it; the threshold carries only the name. The magnitude is enough at this point, because the point is that the community’s obedience is not unrewarded, and the magnitude of the reward is the measure of how seriously the obedience is taken.

**A Promise at the Threshold of the Warning**

The verse’s position is the last thing that gives it its shape. It stands between the command to bear true testimony and the warning that follows, and the pairing of this verse with the next is the sūrah’s way of stating the whole opening block in two sentences. The promise comes first, and the warning comes second, and the two together are the complete answer to the community’s standing. The community is told what its faith and its deeds are worth, and it is told, in the next breath, what the refusal of the same signs is worth. The promise is not softened by the warning, and the warning is not softened by the promise. They stand in the order the sūrah chooses — the good news first, because the sūrah is a sūrah of the community that has been promised the favour — and the two verses together close the opening block with the full statement of where the community stands.

**A Promise the Sūrah Will Cash**

The indefinite reward of this verse is cashed, in the sūrah’s closing section, in its full described form. The community that has believed and borne witness is promised, near the end of the sūrah, exactly the content the threshold verse only named: “So Allah will reward them for what they said with Gardens under which rivers flow, to stay there forever” (5:85). The two forms of the promise — the magnitude at the threshold, the description at the close — are the sūrah’s method. The threshold states that the promise exists and is great, and it does so in the middle of the opening block, where the structure has no room for the description. The close of the sūrah, where the believing community of the People of the Book is named for its witness, has the room, and it gives the reward its full form. The reader who holds the two verses together sees the doctrine of the sūrah in its complete arc: the promise made at the beginning, and the promise kept at the end, with the long history of the covenant and its breaking stretched between the two.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:10
> As for those who disbelieve and deny Our signs, they are the residents of the Hellfire.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Warning That Completes the Promise**

The verse is the second half of a pair. The previous verse stated the promise — forgiveness and a great reward for those who believe and do good — and this verse states the warning in the same economy: a condition in one clause, a destination in one clause, and nothing else. Read together, the two verses are the sūrah’s complete statement of the end, compressed into the length of two sentences. The pairing is deliberate, and it is the sūrah’s way of closing its opening block with the full picture. The community that has been told what its obedience is worth is told, in the same breath, what the refusal of the signs is worth, and the two statements are given equal weight and equal brevity. The warning does not elaborate the promise, and the promise does not soften the warning. They stand side by side, and the side-by-side placement is the doctrine.

**The Stronger Word: Denying the Signs**

The condition of the warning is stronger than a simple statement of disbelief would be. The verse names two acts: disbelief, and the denial of the signs. The first is the absence of the belief; the second is the active rejection of what has been shown. The signs (āyāt) are the verses, the proofs, the revelations that the Book has been sending, and to deny them is not to miss them but to refuse them after they have been presented. The condition of the warning is therefore the condition of the one who has seen and refused, not the one who has not yet seen. This is why the warning carries the weight it does. It is not addressed to the ignorant, and it is not addressed to the weak, but to the one who has been given the signs and has turned them away, and the destination named for that one is the Hellfire, without any qualification or exception. The verse states the hardest case of the doctrine, and it states it plainly.

**Residents, Not Visitors**

The verse names the destination with a word that states permanence: the residents of the Hellfire. The language of residence is the Qur’an’s way of marking the difference between the trials of this world and the state of the next. The world is a place one passes through, and its pains are the pains of the passage; the Hellfire, as the verse states it, is a place one stays. The parallel at 2:39 uses almost the same sentence for the same state: “But those who disbelieve and deny Our signs will be the residents of the Fire” (2:39). The permanence is the point of the warning, and it is what distinguishes the warning from a mere threat. A threat that the listener can outlast is not a warning; the verse removes that option by naming the state as one of residence. The one who denies the signs is not threatened with a pain he will survive; he is told the place where he will remain, and the remaining is the punishment.

**The Symmetry of the Pair**

The two verses together form a symmetry that is the structure of the sūrah’s opening. The promise names two rewards — forgiveness and a great reward — and the warning names one destination, the Hellfire. The asymmetry is not a defect; it is the doctrine. The good news is given in its fullness, with both the removal of the residue and the addition of the prize, because the sūrah is a sūrah of the community that has been promised the favour and is meant to hear the promise in its complete form. The bad news is given in its single, unadorned form, because there is nothing to add to it. The pair states the whole end of the matter in two sentences, and the two sentences are the opening block’s answer to the question the sūrah has been setting up since verse 1: where does the community stand, and what is at stake in the standing?

**A Warning at the Threshold**

The position of the verse is the last thing that gives it its force. It stands at the end of the opening block that addresses the Muslim community as a whole, and immediately after it the sūrah turns to the communities that came before — the Children of Israel and their covenant, the Christians and their covenant — and the warning is the hinge between the two. The community that has been told the promise and the warning is now shown, in the verses that follow, what the breaking of a covenant looks like in the communities that already broke theirs. The warning names the destination, and the history that follows shows the road that leads there. The two parts of the sūrah are joined at this verse, and the joining is the sūrah’s method: the doctrine stated in two sentences, and the doctrine illustrated in the long narrative that follows.

**The Signs the Warning Rests On**

The warning of the verse rests on the thing it names as the object of the denial: the signs. The signs are the verses and the proofs that the Book sends, and the warning is addressed to the one who has seen them and refused them, which is why the condition is stated in the active form, the denial of what has been presented. The signs are the measure of the responsibility, and the warning is proportionate to the measure. The one who has not been shown the signs is not the one this verse addresses, and the one who has been shown them and has denied them is the one the destination is named for. The opening block of the sūrah, in which this verse stands, is itself the sign addressed to the community that hears it, and the warning is part of the sign it warns against being denied. The verse states the doctrine in the form the doctrine takes at its hardest point: the presentation of the proof, the refusal of it, and the destination of the one who refuses, all in a single sentence, with nothing between the refusal and its consequence.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:11
> O believers! Remember Allah’s favour upon you: when a people sought to harm you, but He held their hands back from you. Be mindful of Allah. And in Allah let the believers put their trust.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Final Reminder of the Opening**

The verse is the last invocation of the favour in the sūrah’s opening block. The favour has been named in verse 3, in verse 6, in verse 7, and here in verse 11, and the four invocations together mark the section that speaks to the believers as one body before the sūrah turns to the communities that came before. The repetition is the sūrah’s way of building the community’s memory: the favour is not a single event but a standing fact, and the command to remember it is repeated because the forgetting is a standing danger. The verse that follows this one opens the long narrative of the Children of Israel and their covenant, and the sūrah makes sure, in this last reminder, that the Muslim community’s own favour is fixed in the reader’s mind before the contrast begins. The favour remembered here is the protection, and the protection is the favour that the following verses will show to be the one the other community did not keep. The four invocations of the favour, taken together, are the sūrah’s opening statement of who this community is: the one that was the target and was not struck, and the one whose covenant stands in contrast with the covenants that broke.

**The Hands Withheld**

The favour is stated in a single image: a people sought to stretch their hands against the believers, and God held their hands back. The general reading, which the majority of the exegetes give, is that the people are the idolaters of Makkah, who in the earliest period had the greater numbers and the greater strength, and who sought to crush the young community before it had taken root. The favour is the fact that the crushing did not happen. The community was outnumbered, outarmed, and driven from its home, and the hand that was raised against it was held, and the holding is the favour the verse names. The image of the hand is the Qur’an’s standing image for the power that is about to be exercised and is not, and the verse uses it for the divine power that intervenes between the raised hand and the community it would have struck. The protection is not the absence of the threat; it is the presence of the hand that holds the threat.

**The Bedouin with the Weapon**

The exegetes preserve a more specific report for the occasion of the verse. The Prophet was dozing under a tree, his sword laid aside, when a Bedouin came, seized the weapon, and raised it against him. God prevented the killing, and the weapon stayed in the hand that had no power to use it. The report is the kind the tradition keeps for the verses of protection: a single moment, a single man, a single weapon, and the hand held. Its value is not in the detail but in the form. The favour named in the verse, in the report, becomes an event that could have been overheard, and the event is the proof that the holding of the hand is real and particular, not a general blessing. The community is reminded of the favour by being shown the moment in which the favour was a single act of prevention, and the single act is the favour in its most visible form.

**The Banū Naḍīr and the Wergild**

A second report places the verse at a different moment, and al-Ṭabarī gives this one the preference. The Prophet and the Companions nearest to him went to the tribe of Banū Naḍīr in Madinah to ask for help with the blood-money owed for two men his people had killed by accident. Their chief received the visitors with a hospitality that was only a cover, and a plot was set to kill the unarmed Prophet in his own house. Jibrīl came with the warning of the plot, and the Prophet left before it could be carried out. Al-Ṭabarī’s reason for preferring this reading is structural, and it is the reason that matters. The very next verses take up the story of the Israelites and their breaking of the trust, and a verse about their plotting against the Prophet is the natural hinge into that subject. The favour, on this reading, is the escape from the house where the hands had been stretched, and the hinge is the plot that made the escape necessary.

**A Remembrance for the Whole Community**

The verse is addressed in the plural, and the pronoun that runs through it is the plural pronoun, which is the exegetes’ ground for the reading that the favour is a community favour rather than a biographical note. The hands were stretched against the believers, not against the Prophet alone, and the hand was held for the community, not for the man. The verse is the community’s remembrance of the times it was the target and was not struck, and the remembrance is the favour in the form the community can keep..

**The Favour as the Ground of the Trust**

The closing command — in Allah let the believers put their trust — is the fruit the verse expects from the remembrance. The trust (tawakkul) is not a doctrine about God’s power that the believer repeats; it is a habit formed by the sight of the power at work. The community that has seen the hand held, in the general protection of the years and in the particular moments the reports preserve, has been shown what the trust rests on, and the command to trust is the command to keep resting on what has already been seen. The remembrance and the trust are the two ends of the same act. The memory of the favour is the ground, and the trust on the Giver is the growth, and the verse joins the two in the single breath that closes the opening block. The communities whose story follows in the sūrah are shown, in the narrative that comes, to have had the covenant without the trust, and the trust is the thing that keeps the covenant standing. The verse ends the opening block by placing the community’s habit in the right ground, and the ground is the favour remembered in the four verses that have carried the section from its first command to its last.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:12
> Allah made a covenant with the Children of Israel and appointed twelve leaders from among them and ˹then˺ said, “I am truly with you. If you establish prayer, pay alms-tax, believe in My messengers, support them, and lend to Allah a good loan, I will certainly forgive your sins and admit you into Gardens under which rivers flow. And whoever among you disbelieves afterwards has truly strayed from the Right Way.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Turn to the Cautionary Covenant**

The verse opens the long narrative section of the sūrah, and its function is to set up the contrast the section will develop. The opening block has fixed the Muslim community’s favour and its covenant, and the sūrah now turns to the community that came before, with its own covenant and its own breaking. The pattern is the one the Book uses elsewhere: the covenant with the Children of Israel is named, and the unfaithfulness that followed is told, as in 2:40, 2:63, 2:83, 4:154, and again later in this sūrah at verse 70. The verse is the thesis of the section. A covenant was made, the terms were stated, the promise was given, and the breaking came. The narrative that follows is the evidence, and the verse is the claim the evidence is meant to support. The turn is not a digression from the Muslim community’s affairs; it is the continuation of the sūrah’s argument, by the method of the example that shows what the covenant becomes when it is not kept.

**The Twelve Chieftains**

The verse names twelve leaders (naqībūn) raised among the Israelites, and the exegetes connect them to the twelve tribes into which the community of Moses was divided, as the Book states in 7:160, where the people are divided into twelve tribes, each a community. In the reading the majority follows, the twelve were the trustworthy men chosen by Moses as the representatives of the tribes, sent ahead to scout the land the community was being called to enter, and they returned with the report of the land’s inhabitants that led to the refusal the sūrah will tell in the verses that follow. The representative of the clan of Joseph among the twelve, in the reports the exegetes preserve, is Yūshaʿ ibn Nūn — the Joshua of the Biblical story — and he is identified with one of the two God-fearing men who, in verse 23, remained willing to enter the land against the report of the majority. A minority reading takes the twelve as righteous kings or judges through whom God led the people, and the reading is possible, but the connection to the scouting of the land is the one the structure of the sūrah supports, because the land is where the next verse sends them.

**“I Am Truly With You”**

The first word of the covenant is a promise of presence: I am truly with you. The promise is addressed, in the exegetes’ division, either to the twelve chieftains as the representatives, or to the Israelites and their descendants as the whole, or most likely to both, since the twelve stand for the tribes they represent. The promise of presence is the condition on which the rest of the covenant stands. The terms that follow — the prayer, the alms, the belief in the messengers, the support of the messengers, the loan to God — are the terms of a covenant made with a God who is present, and the presence is what makes the terms binding. The parallel with the Muslim community’s covenant is the sūrah’s quiet point. The community that was told, in verse 7, to remember the covenant and the favour is now shown the covenant in its full, stated form, with the promise of presence at its head, and the form is the standard by which the keeping and the breaking are both measured.

**The Terms of the Covenant**

The terms are named in a list that is the sūrah’s summary of the religion as it was given to the Israelites. The performance of the prayer and the giving of the regular alms are, in the Book’s consistent teaching, the essential practices of the true religious communities, not a private development of one community among many. The belief in the messengers and the support of them is the term that the community broke, and the breaking is the subject of the Book’s standing critique: the denial of the messengers that were to come, and the killing of the messengers that had come, are the failures the Qur’an names again and again against the descendants of the prophets. The loan to God, which closes the list, is the economic form of the devotion, and the four terms together — the ritual, the charity, the belief, the support, the expenditure — are the whole of the covenant as the verse states it. The promise that follows the list is the promise made for the keeping of the whole, not of any part of it.

**The Loan to God**

The last term of the covenant is stated in the Book’s economic language: lend to God a good loan. The loan is the standing metaphor for the expenditure of one’s wealth and effort for the cause of the religion, and the Book uses it in the same sense at 2:245, 57:11, and 64:17. The verse that pairs it with the same duties of the prayer and the alms is 73:20, and the pairing is the sūrah’s way of stating that the expenditure is not an addition to the ritual obligations but a form of them. The loan is a loan, and the language of the loan carries its own doctrine: what is lent is not lost, and the lender is the one who is paid back, and the payment is the promise that follows the list. The covenant is a transaction in which God is the creditor, and the credit is the forgiveness and the Gardens, and the verse states the transaction in the language of the market so that the devotion is not separated from the life in which the devotion is practised.

**The Promise and the Breaking**

The verse closes with the promise and the warning in a single breath. The promise is the forgiveness of the sins and the admission into the Gardens under which rivers flow, and the warning is the fate of the one who disbelieves afterwards: he has strayed from the Right Way. The word “afterwards” is the hinge of the verse. The covenant is a covenant made at a moment, and the breaking is the breaking that comes after the moment, in the life that follows the pledge. The sūrah will show the breaking in the long narrative that follows — the refusal of the land, the distortion of the scripture, the neglect of the terms — and each of those failures is the failure of the one who disbelieved afterwards. The verse is the promise and the warning at once, and the two are held together by the single word that separates the pledge from the life that follows it, and that word is the word the following verses will live inside.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:13
> But for breaking their covenant, We condemned them and hardened their hearts. They distorted the words of the Scripture and neglected a portion of what they had been commanded to uphold. You ˹O Prophet˺ will always find deceit on their part, except for a few. But pardon them and bear with them. Indeed, Allah loves the good-doers.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Breaking as the Stated Reason**

The verse opens with a conjunction that does the work of the whole argument: for breaking their covenant. The punishment that follows is not presented as an arbitrary decree but as the stated consequence of a prior act, and the sūrah has already fixed the covenant in verse 12, so the breaking is the breaking of the specific pact whose terms the reader has just heard. The pattern is the Book’s standing one. The covenant of the Israelites is named and its unfaithfulness told in 2:63, 2:83, 2:93, 4:155, and 7:169, and the verse here belongs to that series. The condemnation (lanāth) is stated in the same register as elsewhere — 2:88, 4:46–47, and again in this sūrah at verses 64 and 78 — and one exegete, al-Zamakhsharī, understands the condemnation as the exile from the mercy of God, which is the strictest form the word takes. The verse keeps the cause and the effect in the order in which they happened: the breaking first, the consequence second, and nothing between the two.

**The Heart That Hardens**

The second consequence of the breaking is stated in the verb that the Qur’an uses for the heart’s loss of receptivity: the hearts were hardened. The idea runs through the Book. God has sealed the hearts and the hearing of the disbelievers, and their sight is covered (2:7), and the hardening of Pharaoh’s heart is the Biblical pattern the Qur’an knows and repeats. The exegetes add an image that explains the metaphor from the material world. Precious metals in their pure state are soft and malleable, and they harden when mixed with other metals into an alloy.
