A heart that is pure is soft to the reminder, and a heart that is mixed with disbelief is hard, and the hardening is the result of the mixture, not a separate punishment imposed from outside. The verse states the hardening as the second effect of the breaking, after the condemnation, and the two effects together — the exile from the mercy and the loss of the softness — are the full consequence the sūrah assigns to the broken covenant.

**Two Senses of the Distortion**

The charge of distortion (taḥrīf) carries two meanings, and the exegetes keep both. In the first sense, the words of the scripture were rearranged, and the rearrangement altered the meaning. In the second sense, the words were left as they were, and the meaning was misrepresented in the interpretation. The verse does not choose between the two, and the structure of the sūrah allows both, because the following verses will attribute to the community both the taking of words out of context and the misreading of the text. The distortion is presented as the visible sign of the hardened heart, since a heart that is still soft to the reminder has no reason to alter the reminder, and the sequence of the verse — the hardening, then the distortion, then the forgetting — is the sequence of the process by which a covenant becomes a letter that is kept in form and broken in substance. The neglect of a portion of what they were reminded of completes the process: the part that was kept is the part that cost nothing, and the part that was dropped is the part that cost.

**The Treachery and the Pardon**

The mention of the treachery the Prophet will find on their part connects, in the reading of the exegetes who make the link, to the incident alluded to in verse 11, the plot of the Banū Naḍīr, and the verse is the community’s instruction for the moment when the treachery is known and the power to punish is in the hand. The command is to pardon and to forbear, and it is issued despite the expectation of the treachery, not in ignorance of it. The parallels are the Book’s standing commands of the same kind: 3:159, where the Prophet is told that forbearance and pardon are of the strength of God, 7:199, the command to adopt the pardon, and 24:22, the command that the one who has power should pardon. The verse places the command of the pardon in the middle of the account of the breaking, which is to say the sūrah does not let the account of the fault generate the act of the revenge, and the pardon is the act the sūrah expects of the community that holds the account in its hand.

**The Question of the Abrogation**

A widely cited report from the early commentator Qatādah holds that the command to pardon was abrogated by 9:29, the verse that commands the fighting of the disbelieving People of the Book until they pay the tax, willingly submitting. The objection to the report is chronological, and it is the one the later commentators press. Sūrah 9 was reported to be revealed in the year 9 AH / 631, while this sūrah was revealed, in its entirety on the common account, during the Farewell Pilgrimage in 10 AH / 632, at the very end of the mission. A later verse does not abrogate an earlier one, and the command to pardon stands as the position of the last revealed word on the subject. The commentators who hold that the two verses are not mutually exclusive give the reason that once the People of the Book have agreed to the standing arrangement, there is no reason the past transgressions cannot be pardoned, and the two commands operate at different levels. The verse, following shortly after the permission to eat the food and to marry the women of the People of the Book, carries the broader intention of this very late sūrah to reestablish the normative and peaceful relations between the two communities, and the pardon is the act that the intention names.

**The Closing Formula of the Lover**

The verse ends with the formula that states the character of the one who gives the command: Allah loves the good-doers. The formula is repeated in the Book at 2:195, 3:134, and again in this sūrah at verse 93, and it appears here at the end of the verse that commands the pardon, which is not a coincidence of placement. The pardon is the act of the one who loves the doers of good, and the command to pardon is grounded in the attribute of the Giver, not in the merit of the one who is pardoned. The community is told to do what God does, and the reason it is told is that God does it because he loves the ones who do it. The verse closes the account of the breaking with the name of the Giver, and the name is the ground on which the pardon stands, and the ground is wider than the fault it covers.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:14
> And from those who say, “We are Christians,” We took their covenant, but they neglected a portion of what they had been commanded to uphold. So We let hostility and enmity arise between them until the Day of Judgment, and soon Allah will inform them of all they have done.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same Covenant, Told Again**

The verse repeats the structure of the previous one, with a different community in the place where the Israelites stood. A covenant was taken from those who say, “We are Christians,” a portion of what they were reminded of was neglected, and a consequence followed. The symmetry is the method of the sūrah. The covenant of the first community and its breaking were told in the verses before, and the covenant of the second community and its breaking are told here, and the two accounts together are the sūrah’s statement that the breaking of the covenant is not a fact that belongs to one people but a possibility that belongs to every people that receives a covenant. The address to those who say, “We are Christians,” is the verse’s way of naming the community by its own self-description, and the self-description is taken as the mark of the community in the way the sūrah takes the marks of the other communities throughout the account.

**A Different Consequence for a Different Failure**

The consequence named in this verse differs from the one named for the Israelites, and the exegetes take the difference to be meaningful. The failure of the first community was met with the condemnation and the hardening of the hearts, a punishment that acts on the individual soul. The failure of the second community is met with the stirring up of hostility and enmity among them, a punishment that acts on the body of the community. The allusion, in the reading the exegetes give, is to the factionalism within the Christian community, the divisions that the theological differences produced, and the verse presents the division as the mode of the punishment, in the same register as 6:65, where the power to split a community into factions and make them taste the violence of one another is named as the torment God can unleash. A less likely reading, which the exegetes note and set aside, takes the enmity as the hostility between the Jews and the Christians, but the verse is addressed to the Christians exclusively, and the enmity that is stirred up is the enmity within the addressed community.

**Until the Day of Judgment**

The verse states the duration of the enmity, and the duration is until the Day of Judgment. The division is not a temporary disorder that the community will outgrow; it is the standing condition of the community from the moment of the neglect to the day of the account, and the statement is the sūrah’s way of saying that the religious differences of this world are not to be resolved in this world. The Book states the same doctrine elsewhere. The Jews and the Christians dispute, each claiming that the other has nothing to stand on, and the Book’s answer is that Allah will judge between them on the Day of Judgment regarding their dispute (2:113), and the same answer closes the verse of the different ways of law at 5:48. The differences remain, providentially, until the clarification that comes at the end, and the verse here names the duration of the Christian division with the same finality. The enmity is the state, and the state is fixed until the day that ends the states.

**The Portion That Was Dropped**

The verse does not name the portion of the reminder that was neglected, and the silence is the sūrah’s method, which is to name the fact of the neglect without fixing the particular. The reminder (dhikr) is the standing term for what God sends to be kept in memory, and the neglect is the human vice that runs through the whole account of the covenant, beginning with the forgetting that belongs to the condition of the human being from the first. The Book tells of the series of reminders that God sends because the forgetting is a standing fact, and the forgetting of the portion is what converts the covenant from a kept trust into a broken one, in the same way the breaking of the covenant in the previous verse converted it. The portion that was kept and the portion that was dropped are not measured in the verse, and the measure is the one the community that neglects a portion will receive on the day it is informed of what it did.

**The Informing That Ends the Dispute**

The verse closes with the promise that God will inform them of what they used to do, and the informing is the end of the enmity as well as the end of the dispute. The enmity that was stirred up among them is the state of the community in this world, and the informing is the act of God in the next, and the two are connected by the single verb that runs through the sūrah, the verb of the informing, which appears at the end of the verse of the covenant and will appear again at the end of the long account. The informing is not a punishment in the form of the torment; it is the act by which the deed is made known to the doer, and the knowledge of the deed is what the one who did it in the state of the enmity has not had in this world. The verse states the division and its duration and its end in a single breath, and the end is the informing, which is the act that ends what the division began.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:15
> O People of the Book! Now Our Messenger has come to you, revealing much of what you have hidden of the Scriptures and disregarding much. There certainly has come to you from Allah a light and a clear Book

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Messenger as the One Who Makes Clear**

The verse opens the address that runs through the middle of the sūrah, and the address is to the People of the Book, the two communities whose covenants and breakings the preceding verses have told. The Messenger has come to them, and his coming is stated in the active voice of the sūrah: he makes clear to them much of what they had hidden of the Book, and he leaves alone much. The two verbs are the verse’s statement of the Messenger’s function. He does not come to add a new scripture beside the ones they hold; he comes to the scripture they hold, and he makes clear the part that was concealed, and he leaves the part that does not need to be made clear. The concealment is the charge, and the making-clear is the act that answers the charge, and the verse states the two together so that the answer is not separated from the fault it answers.

**What Was Hidden**

The exegetes give examples for the parts of the scripture that were hidden, and the examples they give are of two kinds. In the first kind are the passages that referred to the coming of the Messenger, the description of the unlettered Prophet that the Book says they find in their Torah and the Gospel (7:157). In the second kind are the passages that established the law of stoning for the offense of adultery, the law the sūrah elsewhere reports the Prophet applied when the case was brought to him. The charge of the concealment runs through the Book in the verses that name the hiding of the parts of the scripture, 2:42, 2:140, 2:146, 2:159, 2:174, 3:71, 3:187, and 6:91, and the verse here is the address that gathers the charge and states its answer. The concealment, in the sūrah’s account, is the act by which the community that holds the scripture withholds from the community it is meant to reach, and the making-clear is the act by which the witholding is reversed.

**The Light and the Clear Book**

The second sentence of the verse names what has come from God: a light and a clear Book. The light (nūr) is the standing term of the Book for the guidance that comes through the prophets and the revelation, and it appears in the verses that name the guidance sent down, 6:91, 7:157, 21:48, 42:52, and 64:8, and the verse here belongs to that series. The exegetes divide over the referent, and the division is the standard one. On the reading they give first, the light is the Messenger, and the clear Book is the Qur’an, in the sense in which the Book is named the clear Book at the openings of the sūrahs, 12:1, 26:2, 27:1, 28:2, 43:2, and 44:2. On the reading they give second, the light and the clear Book are both the revelation, the light being the quality of the revelation and the clear Book its form. The two readings agree on the fact that the light has come, and the light is the guidance, and the guidance has come to the People of the Book in the person of the Messenger and in the Book that was revealed to him.

**The Book That Is a Light**

The concept of the light that the verse names is the concept the Book builds at its fullest in the verse that states, “Allah is the Light of the heavens and the earth” (24:35). The light is the attribute of the guidance, and the guidance is the act by which the one who is in the darkness is brought into the light, and the Book that is the light is the instrument of the act. The sūrah of the Light names God as the light, and this sūrah names the Book as the light, and the two statements together are the Book’s doctrine of the guidance: the source is the light, and the instrument is the light, and the act by which the creature is moved from the darkness into the light is the act the instrument performs. The verse here states that the light has come, and the coming is the event the sūrah places at the centre of the address to the People of the Book, and the coming is what the following verse will show to be the act of the guidance.

**The Sentence That Continues**

The verse ends in the middle of a sentence. The clear Book is named, and the sentence that the naming opens is completed in the verse that follows, which states what the Book does for the one it reaches. The structure is the sūrah’s way of holding the two verses together as a single statement, the coming of the light and the act of the light, and the reader who holds the two together has the whole of the address: the Messenger has come, making clear what was hidden, and the light has come, and the light is the act by which the one who seeks is guided. The sentence is left open at the end of this verse, and the opening is the hinge on which the next verse turns.

**The Address That Gathers the Account**

The address of this verse gathers the account the sūrah has been telling. The two covenants and the two breakings were told to the reader in the verses before, and the address now turns to the communities themselves and states the event that comes after the breakings: the Messenger has come, and with him the light. The method of the sūrah is the method of the address, which is to tell the community what has happened to it and then to tell it what is coming to it, and the two halves of the method are the two verses, this one and the one that follows. The address is not a reproach that ends in the reproach; it is a reproach that ends in the gift, and the gift is the light that has come, and the coming of the light is the event that the account of the covenants was building to. The reader who has followed the account from the covenant in verse 12 to the address in verse 15 has the whole of the sūrah’s method in view: the covenant, the breaking, the address, and the gift, in the order the sūrah keeps.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:16
> through which Allah guides those who seek His pleasure to the ways of peace, brings them out of darkness and into light by His Will, and guides them to the Straight Path.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sentence Completed**

The verse is the second half of a sentence that began in the verse before. The light and the clear Book were named, and this verse states what the Book does: it is the means by which God guides. The grammar of the continuation is the structure of the sūrah, and the two verses together are the single statement of the address to the People of the Book. The Book is not the end of the address; it is the means, and the means is what the following clause names. The guidance is the act of God, and the Book is the instrument of the act, and the verse states the two in the order in which they stand, the agent first and the instrument second, so that the act is not attributed to the instrument and the instrument is not separated from the act. The sentence that was left open at the end of the previous verse is closed here, and the closing is the statement of the function of the light that was named.

**Those Who Seek His Pleasure**

The guidance is not stated as a general fact; it is stated for a class, and the class is those who seek the pleasure (riḍwān) of God. The condition is the standing one of the Book’s promises, and the most sincere and meritorious of the believers are, in the Book’s consistent teaching, the ones who do the righteous deeds seeking the pleasure of God, as the verses at 2:207, 2:265, 4:114, 5:2, and 59:8 state. The guidance of the verse is for the one who seeks, and the seeking is the act that opens the way to the guidance, and the verse states the two together so that the guidance is not presented as a fate that falls on the one who does not seek. The pleasure of God is the object of the seeking, and the seeking is the work of the one who is guided, and the work and the act of the guidance are joined in the single clause that opens the verse.

**The Ways of Peace**

The first object of the guidance is named in the phrase the ways of peace (subul al-salām). The phrase carries the double sense the exegetes note. In the first sense, the ways are the paths of peace, the ways of life that the guidance opens, and the peace is the state the ways lead to. In the second sense, the ways are the paths to God, since Peace (al-Salām) is one of the Names of God, and the ways that are named after the Name are the ways that lead to the one who is named by it. The plural is the point of the phrase. The guidance does not open a single way; it opens the ways, the multiple paths that the one who seeks may walk, and the multiplicity of the ways is the mercy of the guidance, which is not a narrow path but the opening of the road for the one who walks it in the seeking of the pleasure.

**Out of Darknesses into Light**

The second act of the guidance is the bringing out of the darkness into the light, and the verse uses the plural for the darkness and the singular for the light. The darknesses (ẓulumāt) are the many states of the one who is without the guidance, and the light is the single state of the one who has it, and the plural and the singular are the verse’s image of the difference between the two. The multiplicity of the world without the guidance is the many darknesses, and the oneness of the guidance is the single light, and the movement is from the many into the one. The act is stated elsewhere in the Book in the same form. God brings the believers out of the darkness and into the light, and the guardians of the disbelievers lead them out of the light and into the darkness (2:257), and the same movement is named at 14:1, 14:5, 33:43, 57:9, and 65:11. The act is by His Will, and the Will is the condition that the verse states, so that the movement is not the work of the one who is moved but the act of the one who moves him.

**The Straight Path**

The third act of the guidance is the guiding to the Straight Path, and the path is the standing term of the Book for the life lived in accord with the guidance. The path is the one the opening of the Book asks to be walked, “Guide us along the Straight Path” (1:6), and it is named in the verses that state the life of the guidance, 2:142, 3:101, 6:87, 43:61, and 67:22. The verse closes the sentence with the path, and the closing is the end of the act of the guidance. The one who seeks the pleasure is guided to the ways of peace, is brought out of the darknesses into the light, and is guided to the Straight Path, and the three acts together are the whole of what the light does for the one it reaches, and the whole is the answer the address to the People of the Book was meant to give.

**The Three Acts as One Movement**

The three acts of the guidance, stated in the verse in the order they are stated, are the one movement of the one who is guided, and the order of the acts is the order of the movement. The first act is the opening of the ways of peace, which is the act that removes the wall and sets the road open. The second act is the bringing out of the darknesses into the light, which is the act that moves the one who walks the road from the state of the darkness into the state of the light. The third act is the guiding to the Straight Path, which is the act that keeps the one who has been moved in the state that is the end of the movement. The three acts are not three separate gifts; they are the three stages of the one act of the guidance, and the Will of God is the condition that runs through the stages, since the movement is the act of the one who moves and not the work of the one who is moved. The verse states the movement in the form that the address was meant to give, and the form is the form of the act, which is the form the light takes when it reaches the one who seeks.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:17
> Indeed, those who say, “Allah is the Messiah, son of Mary,” have fallen into disbelief. Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Who has the power to prevent Allah if He chose to destroy the Messiah, son of Mary, his mother, and everyone in the world all together?” To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth and everything in between. He creates whatever He wills. And Allah is Most Capable of everything. Children of Allah?

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Claim the Verse Refutes**

The verse states the claim it refutes in a single sentence: those who say, “Allah is the Messiah, son of Mary,” (5:17), have fallen into disbelief. The claim is the attribution of the divinity to the Messiah, and the verse belongs to the series of the Book’s statements against the claim, the one at 4:171, which names the Messiah as no more than a messenger of Allah, and the ones that follow in this sūrah at verses 72, 75, and 116, and the one at 9:31. The reference to the mother in the verse, which states that God’s power would reach her together with the Messiah and everyone on earth, is the exegetes’ ground for the reading that the verse also answers the claim that would divinize Mary, the claim the sūrah will meet again at verse 116, where the question is put to the Messiah himself about the worship that was asked of him and his mother. The verse takes the claim in its strongest form, the form in which God is identified with the creature, and it refutes the strongest form, which is to say it refutes the forms that are weaker than it.

**The Argument from the Power to Destroy**

The refutation is put in the form of a question, and the question is the argument. Who has the power to prevent Allah if He chose to destroy the Messiah, his mother, and everyone in the world together? The question states the power of God as the power that reaches the creature at will, and the implication is the one the exegetes draw. The Messiah and his mother, together with everyone on earth, are the creations of God, and the creations are subject to the power of the one who created them, and the one who is subject to the power of another is not the one whose power is the power that destroys. The idiom of the question, in the Arabic, is the verb that means to own and, by extension, to control, and the verb is set against the statement that the kingdom belongs to God, since the kingdom is derived from the same root, and the contrast is the contrast between the one who owns nothing of God and the one to whom the kingdom belongs. The refutation is also, in the reading of the exegetes, the implicit answer to the creedal statement that the Messiah was begotten and not made, since the verse states that the Messiah is among the ones God may create and may destroy, and the one who is created is not the one who creates.

**Creation Without a Father**

The verse closes its argument with the statement that God creates whatever He wills, and the statement carries the implication the exegetes draw. The power of creation is not limited to the manner in which the creation usually occurs, and God may create in any manner He wills, including the creation of a human being without a biological father, which is the manner in which the Messiah was created. The creation of the Messiah without the father is the argument against the divinity, not the argument for it, since the manner of the creation is the act of the one who creates, and the one who is created in an unusual manner is the more clearly the creation, not the less. The Messiah and his mother, in their being the creations of God, are not different from the other human beings in the fact of the creation, and the difference of the manner is the sign of the power of the creator, not the sign of the divinity of the created.

**The Theologians’ Refinement**

The commentators who are oriented to the theology add a refinement that the verse allows. The claim as the verse states it, that Allah is the Messiah, is not the actual teaching of the Christian doctrine, which states that the Messiah is God and does not state that God is the Messiah, and the criticism of the verse is directed at the exaggerated understanding of the nature of Christ rather than at the official doctrine. The two exegetes who press the point, al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī, argue that the doctrine is theologically equivalent to the exaggerated understanding, in its attribution of the creative power and the ruling authority to Christ and in its assertion of the incarnation of God in the human form, even if the explicit teaching of most of the Christians is not the identification the verse states. The refinement is the one the sūrah’s argument allows, since the argument is directed at the attribution of the creative power, and the attribution is the fact the doctrine carries, in whatever form it states the form.

**Sovereignty as the Ground of the Refutation**

The verse ends with the statement that belongs to the argument and not to the ornament: to Allah alone belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth and everything between them, and He creates whatever He wills, and He is Most Capable of everything. The kingdom is the ground on which the refutation stands, and the same closing will appear in the verse that follows, where the claim of the sonship is met with the same statement of the kingdom and the same statement of the return. The pair of verses is the sūrah’s method against the claims of the divinity: the claim is stated, the argument from the power is put, and the sovereignty is named as the ground. The ground is the same in both verses, and the sovereignty of God over the heavens and the earth is the fact that no claim of the sonship or the divinity can stand against, and the verse states the fact in the form that answers the claim it refutes.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:18
> The Jews and the Christians each say, “We are the children of Allah and His most beloved!” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Why then does He punish you for your sins? No! You are only humans like others of His Own making. He forgives whoever He wills and punishes whoever He wills. To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth and everything in between. And to Him is the final return.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Claim Both Communities Make**

The verse attributes to the two communities together the claim that the previous verse attributed to one of them in the form of the divinity. The Jews and the Christians each say, “We are the children of Allah and His most beloved” (5:18), and the verse is the only place in the Book where the claim of the sonship is made by both communities in the same breath. The sources of the claim are the ones the exegetes name. In the Christian scriptures, the idea of the children of God is stated explicitly in the Gospels, and in the Hebrew scriptures the people of Israel is named the firstborn son of God, and the kings of the line of David are named the sons of God in the psalms. The report the exegetes preserve from Ibn ʿAbbās places the occasion of the verse in the Madinan Jews, who met the calls of the Prophet and the warnings of the punishment with the assertion that, as the children of God and His beloved ones, they had nothing to fear, and the verse is the answer to the assertion. The claim is taken in the verse as the claim of a special status that excludes the punishment, and the verse refutes the claim in the form the form carries.

**The Counter-Question**

The refutation is put in the form of the question, and the question is the argument, in the same manner the previous verse put its argument. “Why then does He punish you for your sins?” (5:18) The question states the inconsistency the claim carries. If the community is the children of God and His beloved ones, the punishment of the sins is not the act that is done to it, and the community that accepts the punishment of its sins cannot hold the claim of the sonship as the fact that excludes the punishment. The exegetes note that both communities accept the punishment of the sins, the Christians in their teaching of the account, and the Jews in the claim the Book records at 2:80, where some of them say that the Fire will not touch them except for a number of days, and the acceptance of the punishment is the fact that the claim of the sonship cannot stand against. The question is the sūrah’s way of letting the claim be refuted by the belief the claimants themselves hold, and the refutation is internal to the claim, not external to it.

**Mortals of His Creating**

The answer to the question is the statement that removes the claim at its root: “you are only humans like others of His Own making” (5:18). The phrase names the mortality as the fact that bounds the claim, and the bound is the birth and the death over which the mortal has no ultimate control. The statement is the same in kind as the refutation of the previous verse, which removed the claim of the divinity by naming the Messiah and his mother among the creations of God, and the two verses together are the sūrah’s complete answer to the two forms of the claim, the form in which the creature is identified with the creator and the form in which the creature is given the status of the son. The mortality is the fact that both forms share, and the fact is stated in the verse in the plain language of the human being, which is the language that the claim of the sonship is meant to escape.

**The Metaphor the Qur’an Does Not Take at Face Value**

The exegetes are agreed that the claim as the communities make it is not a literal assertion of the divinity for the individual members, and they take it as the metaphorical and genealogical extension of the claims each community makes about its own. The claim of the Christians is the extension of the claim about the sonship of the Messiah, and the claim of the Jews is the extension of the claim about the sonship that their scriptures attribute to the figures of their history, and the exegetes cite the Gospel passage in which the Messiah says to his followers that he is ascending to his Father and their Father as the possible source of the understanding that the verse answers. The theologians connect the claim of the sonship to the claim of the special mercy and the special favour that the communities are reported to have made for themselves in the passages of the Book that record the claims, and the verse answers the two claims together, since the claim of the sonship is the form and the claim of the favour is the content, and the refutation of the form is the refutation of the content.

**Forgiveness and Punishment Belong to Him Alone**

The verse closes with the statement that the previous verse stated, and the closing is the ground of the refutation as it was the ground there. He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills, and to Allah alone belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth and everything between them, and to Him is the final return. The pair of verses ends on the same ground, the sovereignty of God over the creation, and the sovereignty is the fact that the claims of the sonship and the divinity cannot stand against. The forgiveness and the punishment are the acts of the one whose kingdom they are, and the return to Him is the end of the claim, since the claim is made in the world and the account is kept at the end, and the end belongs to the one whose kingdom is the kingdom of the heavens and the earth. The verse states the claim, the question, the answer, and the ground in the order that the argument requires, and the ground is the same ground the sūrah states in the verse before, which is to say the two verses are one argument in two parts.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:19
> O People of the Book! Our Messenger has indeed come to you, making things clear to you after an interval between the messengers so you do not say, “There has never come to us a deliverer of good news or a warner.” Now there has come to you a deliverer of good news and a warner. And Allah is Most Capable of everything.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Interval Named**

The verse names the time between the prophetic missions of Jesus and Muhammad with a word the Book uses for the cessation of the revelation: the interval (fatrah). The term denotes the temporary cessation, either between the different prophets and the different eras of the prophecy, or between the instances of the revelation sent to the Prophet, as the verse at 17:106 states of the Qur’an itself, revealed in stages and in successive revelations. Here the interval is the span between the two major missions, and the verse states the span so that the coming of the Messenger is placed in the series of the missions, not outside it. Some of the commentators hold that no other prophets were sent in the interval, and the position is the one the plain reading of the verse supports. Others name four minor prophets sent in the time between, three in the Judeo-Christian line, perhaps the unnamed messengers of 36:13–14, and a fourth, an Arab prophet named in the reports as Khālid ibn Sinān al-ʿAbsī. The division does not change the fact the verse states, which is that the interval is the time between the missions, and the coming that ends it is the coming of the Messenger to the People of the Book.

**The Claim the Verse Answers**

The verse states the claim it answers in the form the claimants put it: there has never come to us a deliverer of good news or a warner. The reports the exegetes preserve place the claim in some of the Jews of Madinah, who held that no messenger had been sent by God after Moses, and the claim is the form the community takes when it has closed the series of the missions at its own prophet. The verse answers the claim in the form the claim carries. It does not argue against the claim by producing the evidence of the mission; it states the fact that the mission has come, and the fact is the answer, since the claim is a statement about the absence of what is present. The coming of the Messenger is the fact the claim denies, and the verse states the fact in the voice that does not permit the denial, and the denial is the one the verse removes by the statement of the fact.

**The Bearer of Glad Tidings and the Warner**

The verse names the double role of the prophet in the pair that is the standing formula of the Book. The messengers are the bearers of the glad tidings and the warners, and the pair appears in the verses that state the function of the prophecy, 2:213, 4:165, 6:48, and 18:56, and the verse here belongs to the series. The two faces of the role are the two faces of the message. The glad tidings are the promise of the mercy and the reward, and the warning is the statement of the account and the punishment, and the one who bears the two is the one who carries the whole of the message, which is the promise and the threat together. The verse states the pair at the end of the address, where the coming of the Messenger is confirmed, and the confirmation is the confirmation of the double role, which is to say the Messenger who has come is the one who carries both faces of the message, and the community that receives the one without the other receives the message incomplete.

**Clarity as the Purpose of the Mission**

The verse states the function of the Messenger in the address: he has come making things clear to the People of the Book. The clarity is the purpose of the prophecy in the Book’s doctrine, and the doctrine is stated in the verses that name the making-clear as the act of the messenger. The Messenger makes clear that wherein the communities differ, as the verses at 16:64 and 43:63 state of Muhammad and Jesus respectively, and the address of this sūrah, which states that the Messenger makes clear much of what the People of the Book had hidden of the Book (5:15), is the instance of the doctrine. The purpose of the mission is the removal of the obscurity that the human error and the human falsification have put in the place of the clarity, and the coming of the Messenger is the act by which the obscurity is removed and the clarity is restored. The verse states the function in the middle of the address, and the function is the reason the coming is stated, since the coming is not the end of the mission but the means of the clarification.

**A Chain Without a Gap**

The verse closes with the statement that the chain of the missions is the work of the one who is Most Capable of everything, and the statement is the ground of the fact the verse states. The interval is the time between the missions, and the interval is real, and the fact of the interval is the fact the claim of the gap depends on, but the interval is not the gap, since the chain that the interval lies in is the chain of the one who creates whatever He wills and whose power reaches every span of the time. The verse states the interval, the coming that ends it, and the power that keeps the chain, in the order that the argument requires, and the order is the order of the sūrah’s method, which is to state the fact, the answer, and the ground, in that order, and to leave the claim without the ground it needs.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:20
> And ˹remember˺ when Moses said to his people, “O my people! Remember Allah’s favours upon you when He raised prophets from among you, made you sovereign, and gave you what He had never given anyone in the world.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Moses Reminding His Own People**

The verse opens the narrative of the Holy Land, and the narrative is told in the form of the address of the prophet to his own people. Moses speaks to the Israelites, and his speech is the reminder of the favour, in the same form the sūrah has used for the Muslim community in its opening block, where the believers are told to remember the favour of God in verses 3, 6, 7, and 11. The form is the sūrah’s standing one. The community that has received the covenant is reminded of the favour that came with the covenant, and the reminder is the act that precedes the test, since the community that remembers the favour is the community that is ready for the command that follows. The address of Moses to his people is the instance of the form in the narrative, and the form is the same form the sūrah addresses to the reader, which is to say the narrative is told in the voice that the sūrah uses for its own address, and the reader who hears the address of Moses hears the address of the sūrah in the form it is meant to be heard.

**The Prophets and the Sovereignty**

The favour is stated in two parts, and the first part is the raising of the prophets from among the people. The prophets who were appointed among them are, in the exegetes’ account, Abraham and the other prophets of the line of his descendants, and the fact of the prophets among the people is the first of the favours, since the people that has the prophets among its own is the people that God has chosen for the nearness of the guidance. The second part is the making of the people sovereign, and the sovereignty is, in the reading the exegetes give, the sovereignty over their own affairs, which God granted them when He freed them from the tyranny of Pharaoh. The two parts of the favour are the two marks of the chosen people. The prophets are the mark of the guidance, and the sovereignty is the mark of the freedom, and the people that has both is the people the verse names, and the naming is the reminder that Moses puts in the mouth of the prophet.

**That Which He Gave to No One Else**

The second statement of the favour is the one the verse carries, and it is the statement of the unique gift. God gave the Israelites that which He had never given to anyone in the world, and the statement belongs to the series of the Book’s statements of the unique favour of the Israelites, the verses at 2:47, 2:122, 7:140, and 45:16. The exegetes divide over the content of the gift, and the division is the standard one. On the reading they give first, the gift is the manna and the quails that sustained the people in the desert, as the verses at 2:57, 7:160, and 20:80 state, the last of which tells the Children of Israel that God saved them from their enemy and sent down to them manna and quails. On the reading they give second, the gift is the many prophets and the signs that were sent to them. On the reading they give third, the gift is the many good things of this world and the next that were granted to them. The commentators add the qualification that the unique favour was enjoyed by the Israelites in their time, which is to say the favour was the favour of the period, not the favour that outlasted the people who received it.

**A Reminder Placed Before the Test**

The position of the verse in the narrative is the thing that gives it its function. The favour is stated in this verse, and the command that follows is the command to enter the land in the verse that comes after, and the refusal that follows the command is the refusal the narrative is told to show. The structure of the narrative is the structure of the sūrah’s method. The favour is reminded, the command is given, and the refusal is reported, and the three parts together are the account of the people that had the favour and received the command and did not keep it. The reminder of the favour is not the ornament of the narrative; it is the ground of the account, since the account is the account of the breaking of the trust, and the trust is the trust that the favour established, and the favour is stated in the verse so that the breaking that follows is seen as the breaking of what was given, not the breaking of what was never held.

**The Favour in the Form of the Covenant**

The favour that Moses reminds his people of is the favour that stands in the form of the covenant, and the form is the form the sūrah has already stated for the community it addresses. The covenant of verse 12 is the covenant that carries the terms of the prayer, the alms, the belief in the messengers, the support of the messengers, and the loan to God, and the favour of this verse is the favour that came with the covenant before the terms were stated, which is to say the favour is the ground of the covenant, and the covenant is the form of the favour. The reminder of the favour is the reminder of the ground, and the ground is the thing the community stands on when the command comes, since the command is the command that the ground supports, and the community that has forgotten the ground is the community that stands without the support when the command is given. The verse places the reminder in the mouth of the prophet, and the placing is the sūrah’s way of stating that the reminder of the favour is the work of the prophet, and the work is the work that the community is meant to receive as the work that prepares it for the command.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:21
> O my people! Enter the Holy Land which Allah has destined for you ˹to enter˺. And do not turn back or else you will become losers.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Land That Was Destined**

The command of the verse is the command to enter the Holy Land, and the land is named in the verse with the name the Book gives it. The exegetes connect the land, which is described in the verse as the one God has destined for the Israelites, to Abraham, of whom the reports state that he was shown the land and that it was given to his progeny as the inheritance, which from the Islamic point of view includes his Arab Muslim descendants through the line of Ishmael. The Book describes the land as blessed in a universal way, and the universality of the blessing is stated in the verses that name the blessed land, the verse at 17:1, which mentions the Farthest Mosque, the site of the ancient Temple, and says of its surroundings that they were blessed, and the verse at 21:71, which tells of the delivery of Abraham and Lot to the land that was blessed for all peoples. The term the Holy Land is used in the verse in the sense that is the common sense of the term in the Judeo-Christian context, and the exegetes note that the term is rare in the Bible itself, which is to say the Book takes the term in the sense the term carries in the world it addresses.

**The Location the Reports Do Not Fix**

The exact location of the land the verse names is the question the reports do not settle. The various reports place it in Sinai, in Syria, at Jericho, or in the area that encompasses parts of Syria, Palestine, and Jordan, and some of the exegetes identify it simply as the land of the sanctuary of Jerusalem. Al-Ṭabarī takes the position that the exact location is not knowable, and he places it somewhere between the two rivers, the Euphrates and the Nile, without holding that it is the whole of the area between them. The division of the reports is the division the verse allows, since the verse names the land by its character and not by its boundaries, and the character is the character of the land that was destined, and the boundaries are the matter of the reports that differ. The command of the verse is the command to enter the land as the land is named, and the naming is the destiny, which is the word the verse uses for the act of God that fixed the right of the people to the land.

**The Destiny Written in the Tablet**

The verb the verse uses for the destiny of the land carries the sense the exegetes note. The word can mean to prescribe and it can mean to inscribe, and the reading that some of the commentators give is that the right of the Israelites to the land was inscribed in the Preserved Tablet, the repository of the eternal knowledge and the revelation of God, which the Book names at 85:22 as the tablet that is preserved. On that reading, the command to enter the land is not the command of the moment but the command of the decree, and the decree is the one that was written before the moment, and the entry is the act by which the community fulfils the decree that was made for it. The two readings, the prescription of the moment and the inscription of the tablet, are the two senses of the same verb, and the verse keeps both, since the command is the command of God, and the command of God is the command that is made in the moment and written in the eternity.

**Do Not Turn Back**

The second part of the command is the warning that closes the verse, and the warning carries the double sense the exegetes give. In the first sense, the command not to turn back is the command not to retreat from the land without fighting for it, and the turning back is the act of the one who leaves the land he was commanded to enter. In the second sense, the command is the command not to turn the back on the divine command and the obedience to the prophet, and the turning back is the act of the one who refuses the command in the moment it is given. The warning names the consequence of the turning back, which is the loss, and the loss is the loss of the one who is commanded to enter and does not enter, and the one who turns his back on the command and does not keep it. The verse closes with the warning, and the warning is the statement of what the refusal costs, and the cost is the cost the narrative that follows will show in the form of the forty years.

**A Command That Precedes the Refusal**

The structure of the verse is the structure of the narrative that follows. The command is stated in this verse, and the reply that follows is the reply of the refusal in the verse that comes after, and the two together are the opening of the account that the sūrah tells in the verses that run to verse 26. The biblical counterpart of the account is the narrative of the scouting of the land in Numbers 13–14, and the sūrah’s account is the account of the same event in the form the Book tells the events of the scripture it confirms. The function of the narrative in the sūrah is the function of the cautionary tale, which is the form the Book uses when it tells the events of the communities that came before, and the community that hears the account is the community the sūrah addresses, and the account is the account the community is meant to hear as the account of its own possibility, since the situation the narrative shows is the situation the community can be in, and the refusal the narrative reports is the refusal the community can make.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:22
> They replied, “O Moses! There is an enormously powerful people there, so we will never ˹be able to˺ enter it until they leave. If they do, then we will enter!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Twelve Who Went Ahead**

The reply of the verse is the reply of the community to the command, and the reply is reported in the form that connects it to the twelve who went ahead. After the command to enter the land, in the account the exegetes give, Moses chose the twelve men, one from each of the twelve tribes, to enter the land in advance and to report on its inhabitants, and the twelve are the twelve chieftains the sūrah named in verse 12, which is to say the narrative of the sūrah keeps the thread of the twelve from the covenant to the scouting. While they were in the land, the advance party saw the size and the power of the inhabitants, and the reports the exegetes preserve add the detail that the party was discovered and seized by one of the inhabitants of the land, and was released after, so that the men could warn their own people not to enter. The reply of the verse is the report of the twelve, stated in the voice of the community that received the report, and the community takes the report as the ground of its refusal.

**The Word for the Power They Saw**

The power of the inhabitants is named in the verse with a word that carries the weight the exegetes note. The people are described as the overwhelmingly powerful ones, and the word that is used, when it is applied to a human being, has the sense of the tyrant, the one of the overwhelming force who is able to compel the others to do his will. The word is also one of the Names of God, as the verse at 59:23 states, and the Name conveys the power of God over all the others. When the term is applied to the human being, it takes the negative sense, since the exercise of the power of the compulsion over the others belongs rightly to God alone, and the human being who claims it is the one who takes the Name for himself. The report of the twelve is the report of the power that is described in the language that belongs to the one it imitates, and the language is the mark of the report, which is to say the report is not the report of the size alone but the report of the power that is the size in the form of the compulsion.

**The Condition That Was the Refusal**

The reply states the condition on which the entry depends, and the condition is the one that the narrative shows to be the refusal. The community will not enter until the inhabitants leave, and if they leave, the community will enter. The condition is the statement of the entry that is never the entry, since the inhabitants do not leave, and the condition is the form the refusal takes when the refusal is stated as the caution. The community does not say that it will not enter because it will not fight; it says that it will enter when the way is clear, and the way is never clear, and the caution is the refusal in the form that the refusal does not name itself. The assurance of Moses, which the sūrah states in the covenant of verse 12, that the community that keeps the terms of the covenant will be led to the victory, is the assurance the report of the twelve sets against, and the setting against is the act by which the community chooses the report over the assurance, and the choice is the breaking of the trust that the covenant established.

**A Report That the Community Will Hear Again**

The reply of the verse is the one the sūrah will meet again in the verse that follows, where the refusal is stated in its final form, and the two replies together are the account of the community that heard the command and did not keep it. The understanding the Companions gave to the narrative, in the reports the exegetes preserve, is the understanding that the account is the warning to the community that hears it, the warning to follow the command of the Prophet into the encounter with the stronger power, and not to take the caution for the refusal. The report of the twelve is the report the community can make in the moment of the encounter, and the sūrah tells the report in the form that the community is meant to hear it as the report of its own possibility, since the situation the narrative shows is the situation the community stands in, and the reply the narrative reports is the reply the community can give.

**The Fact and the Choice**

The report of the twelve is the fact, and the reply of the community is the choice, and the verse keeps the two apart in the way that the account requires. The fact is the size and the power of the inhabitants, and the fact is the fact that the twelve saw in the land and reported to the people, and the fact is not the refusal, since the fact is the ground on which the refusal is made, and the ground is not the act. The choice is the choice of the community that receives the fact and puts the fact in the form of the condition, and the putting is the act by which the fact becomes the refusal. The sūrah tells the two in the two verses, the fact in this verse and the choice in the verse that follows, and the telling of the two apart is the method of the account, which is to show the community that the fact is not the excuse, since the fact is the fact that the two men of the verse after received and did not put in the form of the condition, and the two are the measure of the community in the moment of the fact.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:23
> Two God-fearing men—who had been blessed by Allah—said, “Surprise them through the gate. If you do, you will certainly prevail. Put your trust in Allah if you are ˹truly˺ believers.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Two Against the Majority**

The verse names the two who answered the report of the twelve, and the answer is the answer that stands against the majority. Of the twelve who went ahead to scout the land, the two remained confident in the power of God to grant the victory over the inhabitants, and the two tried to calm the fear of the community and to put the confidence in the ability to overcome, with the help of God. The exegetes identify the two with the figures of the biblical account, Joshua, whose name in the reports is Yūshaʿ ibn Nūn, and Caleb, whose name is Kālib ibn Yūfannah, and the identification is the one that accords with the account of the scripture the Book confirms. A minor report, which the exegetes note, takes the two as two of the inhabitants of the land who had turned to the belief in the prophethood of Moses and had returned with the advance party, and the report is the one the identification of the majority does not allow, since the two are stated in the verse as the two of the twelve, and the twelve are the twelve of the Israelites.

**The Blessed as the Mark of the State**

The verse describes the two with a phrase that states their state before it states their act. They are the two whom God had blessed among the God-fearing, and the blessing is the mark of the state, not the reward of the act, since the blessing is stated in the description that precedes the command the two give. The two are selected from among the God-fearing, and the selection is the fact the verse states, which is that the confidence that stands against the fear of the community is the confidence of the one who is marked by the fear of God, and the mark is the mark that the community that fears does not have in the form the two have it. The blessing and the fear are the two marks of the two, and the two marks together are the description that the verse gives before it gives the words, and the words are the words of the one who is described, which is to say the command the two give is the command of the blessed who fear, and the command is not the command of the majority that fears without the blessing.

**The Gate and the Certainty of the Victory**

The command the two give is stated in the concrete form of the act. The community is told to enter upon the inhabitants by the gate, and the gate is the threshold of the land, the point of the entry that the command names. The promise that follows the command is the certainty of the victory, and the certainty is stated in the form of the consequence. Once the entry is made, the victory follows, and the following is the fact the two state, since the two are the ones who have seen the state of the inhabitants and the state of the community, and the two state the certainty in the form that the certainty takes for the one who trusts. The command and the promise are the two parts of the answer, and the answer is the answer that the refusal of the community sets against, and the setting against is the act by which the community chooses the report over the command, and the choice is the choice the narrative is told to show.

**The Trust That Is the Test of the Belief**

The closing words of the two are the words that state the test. Put your trust in God, if you are believers. The trust is stated in the form of the condition, and the condition is the one that the verse uses for the belief, which is to say the trust is the mark of the belief, and the one who is the believer is the one who trusts, and the one who does not trust is the one who is not the believer in the form the belief requires. The two men put the test in the form that the test takes in the moment of the encounter, and the test is the test that the community fails, since the community that answers the trust with the condition of the caution is the community that does not trust in the form the trust requires. The verse states the two and their words, and the words are the words of the trust, and the trust is the thing the narrative that follows will show to be the thing the community did not keep, and the not keeping is the breaking that the sūrah tells in the verses that run to the end of the account.

**The Two in the Account of the Scripture**

The account of the two is the account the sūrah tells in the form that the scripture it confirms tells, and the form is the form of the scouting of the land in the narrative of Numbers, where the twelve go ahead and the two stand against the ten. The sūrah’s account keeps the form of the scripture, and the keeping is the confirmation that the sūrah states of the scripture it reveals, which is to say the account is the account of the same event in the voice of the Book that confirms the book of the community that received the event. The identification of the two with the figures of the scripture is the exegetes’ ground for the keeping, and the ground is the ground that the form of the account supports, since the form of the account is the form that names the two in the place where the scripture names them. The sūrah tells the account without the comment, and the comment is the one the form carries, which is that the two are the two of the scripture and the two of the sūrah, and the two accounts are the one account told in the two voices that the Book keeps.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:24
> ˹Yet˺ they said, “O Moses! ˹Still˺ we will never enter as long as they remain there. So go—both you and your Lord—and fight; we are staying right here!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Words That End the Argument**

The verse states the final form of the refusal, and the final form is the form that ends the argument. The community will not enter as long as the inhabitants remain, and the command to Moses is the command that the refusal puts in the mouth of the one who refuses. Go, you and your Lord, and fight, and we will stay here. The words are the refusal in its complete form, since the refusal that was stated as the caution in the previous verse is stated here as the sitting, and the sitting is the act that the caution becomes when the caution is kept without the entry. The exegetes note the form of the address, that Moses is told to go with his Lord, and the note is the one that keeps the words in the form the words carry. God is not said to go in the physical sense, and the address is the address of the one who has separated the prophet from the help of the one who sent him, and the separation is the act of the refusal, which is to say the community that tells its prophet to go with his Lord is the community that has put the prophet in the place of the one who fights alone, and the fighting alone is the state the refusal assigns to the messenger.

**The Sentence the Community Would Not Say**

The understanding the Companions gave to the narrative is the understanding that the account is the warning to the community that hears it, and the warning is the warning against the form of the refusal that the verse states. The report the exegetes preserve places the understanding in the moment of al-Ḥudaybiyyah, when the Prophet and the Companions were barred from entering Makkah to perform the pilgrimage and stood in the dangerous confrontation with the stronger power of the Meccans in 6 AH / 628. The Companion Miqdād ibn al-Aswad cited the verse of the sūrah and said, by God, we will not be like the party of the Israelites, who said to their prophet, go with your Lord and fight, and we will stay here. The form he put in the place of the refusal is the form of the keeping, we will say, go with your Lord and fight, and we will fight with you, and the two forms are the two answers the narrative shows, the answer of the sitting and the answer of the fighting with. A variation of the report connects the understanding to the moment of the battle of Badr in 2 AH / 624, and the two moments are the two occasions on which the community was put in the situation the narrative shows, and the report is the report of the community that heard the account and did not take the refusal for its own answer.

**The Sitting as the Choice**

The words that close the verse are the words that state the choice. We will stay here. The sitting is the act that the refusal takes when the refusal is the act, and the sitting is the state of the one who has left the place of the entry and kept the place of the safety. The two men of the verse before were told to enter by the gate, and the gate is the threshold of the entry, and the community of this verse is the community that keeps the place it is in, and the keeping is the sitting, and the sitting is the choice that the narrative names. The contrast is the contrast the sūrah states without the comment. The two who enter are the two who trust, and the many who stay are the many who do not trust, and the trust and the sitting are the two states that the narrative shows in the two verses, and the two verses together are the account of the division of the community in the moment of the test.

**A Story That Is an Address to the Reader**

The function of the narrative in the sūrah is the function of the address that the sūrah keeps. The account of the twelve, the two, and the sitting is the account the community that hears it is meant to take as the account of its own situation, since the situation the narrative shows is the situation of the community that is commanded to meet the stronger power and hesitates, and the refusal the narrative reports is the refusal the community can make in the moment of the hesitation. The sūrah tells the account in the form that the account takes when it is the warning, and the warning is the warning that the report of Miqdād shows to be the warning the community received and kept, since the community that heard the account put the form of the keeping in the place of the form of the sitting, and the putting is the act by which the narrative did its work. The verse states the sitting, and the sitting is the state the narrative names, and the naming is the warning that the sūrah gives to the community it addresses.

**The Fear and the Trust as Two Answers to Power**

The narrative shows the two answers that the community can give to the stronger power, and the two answers are the fear and the trust, and the two are the two states that the sūrah keeps in the two verses that meet in this account. The community that answers the power with the fear is the community that puts the power in the form of the condition, and the condition is the form that the fear takes when the fear is stated as the caution. The two men who answer the power with the trust are the two who put the power in the form of the fact, and the fact is the form that the trust takes when the trust is stated as the certainty of the victory. The sūrah keeps the two answers in the account, and the keeping is the method of the teaching, which is to show the community the two answers and to let the community choose in the moment of its own encounter, since the account is the account that the community is meant to hear as the account of the two answers, and the two answers are the two answers the community can give in the situation the narrative shows.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:25
> Moses pleaded, “My Lord! I have no control over anyone except myself and my brother. So set us apart from the rebellious people.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prophet Who Limits His Own Power**

The verse states the plea of Moses in the moment after the refusal of his people, and the plea begins with the statement of the limit. Moses has no control over anyone except himself and his brother, and the statement is the statement of the prophetic authority in its honest form. The prophet is not the one who compels the community to keep the command; the prophet is the one who conveys the command and stands with the community that keeps it, and the limit is the limit the sūrah states without the apology. The plea that follows the statement is the plea for the separation, which is the plea for God to set Moses and his brother apart from the rebellious people, and the separation is, in the reading the exegetes give, the plea for the judgment between the two sides, the side of the prophet and the side of the people who refused. The verse states the limit and the plea together, and the two together are the form of the prophet’s address to God in the moment of the failure of the community.

**The Brother Named in the Plea**

The brother who stands with the prophet in the plea is Aaron, and the exegetes note the place the brother holds in the statement of the limit. Al-Zamakhsharī gives two accounts for the place. In the first, Moses, in his despair with the people, is not sure of the loyalty of any of them except the loyalty of his brother, who is a fellow prophet, and the brother is the one whose keeping of the command Moses is sure of. In the second, the word brother is used in the sense of the religion, and it names all those who follow the prophet faithfully in the religion, not only the brother of the blood. The two accounts are the two senses of the word in the plea, and the sūrah keeps both, since the plea is the plea of the prophet who stands with the one who is sure, and the one who is sure is the brother of the blood and the brother of the faith. The two men who stood against the refusal in the verse before, the two who trusted, are not named in the plea, and their place in the narrative is the place of the two who kept the command, which is the place the plea of the prophet does not reach, since the prophet’s plea is the plea for the separation from the people who did not keep it.

**The Plea as the Request for the Judgment**

The plea for the separation is the request for the judgment, and the request is the form the prophet’s address takes when the community has refused the command. The exegetes understand the separation as the plea for God to judge between Moses and his brother on the one side and the rebellious people on the other, and the judgment is the act that the plea asks for, since the prophet cannot judge the people who have refused him, and the judgment is the act of the one who sent the prophet. The plea is not the plea of the prophet who accuses his people; it is the plea of the prophet who asks for the division, and the division is the act by which the prophet is set apart from the people he could not lead. The verse states the plea in the form that the plea takes in the moment of the refusal, and the form is the form of the prophet’s address to God, which is the form the sūrah keeps for the reader.

**The Plea Stated Without the Apology**

The sūrah keeps the plea in the account without the apology that the later reader might expect, and the keeping is the method of the narrative. The prophet is shown in the moment of the frustration with his people, and the frustration is stated in the form of the plea, and the plea is the form the frustration takes when it is the address to God. The sūrah does not soften the plea, and the not softening is the way the account keeps the prophet in the form the prophet is, which is the form of the one who is sent and not the one who compels, and the one who is grieved by the refusal of the people and asks for the separation from them. The keeping of the plea is the keeping of the narrative in its fullness, and the fullness is the form that the sūrah uses for the accounts of the prophets, which is to state the words of the prophet as they are stated, and to let the reader take the measure of the prophet from the words.

**The Plea That Precedes the Judgment**

The plea of this verse is the plea that the verse after answers, and the two verses together are the exchange between the prophet and God in the moment of the refusal. The plea asks for the separation, and the answer that follows is the statement of the judgment that the plea asked for, which is to say the narrative keeps the two verses as the question and the answer, and the question is the question of the prophet who has done his part and asks for the part that is not his. The structure of the two verses is the structure of the sūrah’s account of the refusal, and the account is the account that runs from the command in verse 21 to the judgment in verse 26, with the plea of the prophet in the middle, at the point where the prophet’s part ends and the part of God begins. The verse states the plea, and the plea is the mark of the point in the narrative where the prophet’s work is done and the judgment is the act that remains.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:26
> Allah replied, “Then this land is forbidden to them for forty years, during which they will wander through the land. So do not grieve for the rebellious people.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Land That Was Closed**

The answer of the verse is the statement of the judgment that the plea of the previous verse asked for, and the judgment is the closing of the land. The land is forbidden to the people for forty years, and the forty years are the period of the wandering, and the wandering is the state of the people in the time that the land is closed. The exegetes divide over the sense of the forbidding, and the division is the standard one. In the first sense, the people were prevented in the physical fact from entering the land, and the forbidding is the act by which the entry was closed to them. In the second sense, the people were to keep the forbidding as the matter of the devotion and the submission to God, and the forbidding is the command that they were to observe. The two senses are the two forms of the closing, and the sūrah keeps both, since the closing is the act of God and the command of God, and the two are the two faces of the one act.

**The Reading That Makes the Exile Last**

The opening of the verse carries the reading that some of the exegetes give, and the reading is the one that makes the exile the exile that does not end in the entry. The verse can be read as the statement that the land is forbidden to them, full stop, and that the forty years of the wandering are the statement of the fact that they will most likely die in the wandering before they enter. On that reading, the exile is the exile of the disobedient among the Israelites, and the exile is the state that the generation that refused keeps until the generation that refused is gone. The account of the scripture that the sūrah confirms states the same fact, and the narrative of the book of the community that received the account is the account that the sūrah’s reading accords with. The two readings, the forty years as the term of the closing and the forty years as the fact of the death of the generation, are the two senses of the same number, and the number is the number the sūrah states for the judgment.

**The Ordinance That Remained in Suspension**

The exegetes note that the ordinance of the land for the people remained, even in the time of the closing. The right of the people to the land, which the verse of the command stated as the act of God that was inscribed in the Preserved Tablet, was not taken by the judgment; it was held in the state of the suspension. The suspension is the form the ordinance takes in the time of the exile, and the form is the form that the decree keeps when the decree is not fulfilled in the moment. The entry that came after the wandering is the entry of the next generation, and the reports state that the city of Jericho was taken under the leadership of the man whom the exegetes consider the prophet sent to the people after the death of Moses, and some of the reports hold that the prophet of the sūrah entered the city with him and slew one of the giants of the inhabitants. The two facts, the ordinance in the suspension and the entry of the generation that kept, are the two parts of the account that the sūrah keeps in the form it states.

**The Command Not to Grieve**

The answer closes with the command to the prophet, and the command is the command that the plea of the previous verse makes necessary. The prophet is told not to grieve for the rebellious people, and the exegetes state the reason the command is given. The prophet was grieved by the severity of the judgment that came as the answer to his plea, and he regretted the plea that had asked for the judgment, and the command is the act by which God sets the prophet in the state that the judgment requires. The not grieving is the state of the one who has done his part and taken the judgment in the form the judgment is, and the state is the state the sūrah names for the prophet in the moment after the plea. The command is the second part of the answer, and the answer is the judgment and the consolation together, which is to say the sūrah states the severity of the judgment and the care for the prophet who asked for it in the same breath.

**The Wandering That Was Sustained**

The exegetes take up the question of the state of the prophet and his brother in the time of the wandering, and the question is the one the plea of the previous verse raises, since the prophet asked to be set apart from the people who were to wander. Most of the accounts hold that the two remained with the people in the time of the exile, and some of the accounts state that the exile was not the hardship for the two, in the manner the fire that was ordered to be cool and safe for the man who was cast into it (21:69) was not the burning for the one it was ordered for. The accounts add that God continued the comfort and the sustenance for the people in the exile, and the sustenance is the water that gushed from the twelve springs when the prophet struck the rock with his staff, as the verses at 2:60 and 7:160 state, and the manna and the quails that the verse at 7:160 names. The wandering is the state of the punishment, and the sustenance is the act of the provision that the punishment does not take, and the two together are the form the sūrah keeps for the account of the exile.

---

## Sūrah al-Māʾidah 5:27
> Relate to them in truth ˹O Prophet˺ the story of Adam’s two sons—how each offered a sacrifice: Abel’s offering was accepted while Cain’s was not. So Cain threatened, “I will kill you!” His brother replied, “Allah only accepts ˹the offering of the sincerely devout.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Relate the Account**

The verse opens the account of the two sons of Adam with the command to the Prophet, and the command is the command to relate the account in the truth to the community the sūrah addresses. The exegetes note the sense of the command in the context of the sūrah. The account is to be related to the Jews and to the idolaters, and the occasion that some of the commentators give is the occasion the sūrah states in the verse of the hands stretched against the community, which is the plot against the Prophet and his Companions that the sūrah told at the close of its opening block. The story is about the moral danger of the spiritual envy, and the envy is the envy the sūrah states in the verses that name the resentment of the earlier communities for the grace given to the community of the Prophet, and the account is the account the community is meant to hear as the account of the envy that kills.
